center soc ../security operations center soc — consuming sase logs and alerts15:T1542,smart contracts and decentralized apps smart contracts, wallets, tokens, and dapp frontends — how programmable logic and self-custody put application state on an open, incentive-aligned blockchain. developer frontend backend tester solution-architect security-architect web3 smart-contracts tokens wallets decentralization blockchain  what this system is smart contracts are programs deployed onto a blockchain that execute deterministically on every node — code becomes a shared, verifiable public api. a decentralized application dapp is the combination of a smart contract the back end, living on chain and a normal web front end off chain , plus a wallet the user uses to sign transactions. where a traditional app trusts a server, a dapp trusts everyone running the contract . there is no admin account that can edit the ledger's history for a public chain; rules are enforced by the consensus rules described in blockchain fundamentals ../blockchain fundamentals , not by an operator. what problem it solves classic flow dapp counterpart a server holds a database of balances and a ledger balances and state live in the contract's storage, visible to all back end logic can be changed or hot patched at will deployed on chain code is immutable; upgrades are explicit and auditable users trust the operator not to freeze or censor them no operator can silently censor a transaction that pays the fee ownership is "whoever controls the server" ownership is "whoever controls the private keys" self custody the trade off is real: the same transparency that removes the operator a trust also removes user privacy everyone can read all balances and places strict limits on throughput, so contracts keep only essential logic and state on chain. anatomy of a transaction tokens: fungible and non fungible token type example standard behavior fungible erc 20 ethereum every unit is interchangeable — currency, staking rewards, governance votes non fungible nft erc 721 each token is unique — deeds, collectibles, digital art, licenses semi fungible / soulbound erc 1155 / custom mixes or non transferable credentials — the bridge to decentralized identity ../decentralized identity and verifiable credentials a token contract is itself just a smart contract keeping a mapping of addresses to balances, with rules like emit an event, transfer, approve, and mint/burn. any contract or wallet can read those transfers — which is why data pipelines etl/elt ../data pipelines etl elt indexers exist to normalize on chain events into analytics tables. wallets and key custody self custody : the wallet holds the private keys; losing them is losing the funds. no "i forgot my password — reset me" path exists. seed phrase : keys are derived from a bip 039 mnemonic; it is the account, so it must be handled like secrets, certificates and pki ../secrets certificates and pki secrets. hierarchical deterministic hd wallets : one seed derives many addresses, so a dapp can request sub accounts per session. contracts as wallets : multi sig and smart wallets enforce rules like "need 2 of 3 signers" — moving account policy from a human to code. when dapps make sense open financial primitives — lending, swaps, stablecoins where transparency and composability beat operator trust. governance and daos — tokens give votes, and the treasury is a contract, so spending is verifiable by anyone. protocols that other developers build on — composability: your contract can call my contract, both serving the same shared state. self custody products — users genuinely control their assets rather than a company's account table. when in doubt, prefer a normal app: dapps trade performance, privacy, and upgradeability for decentralization. if the "trust third party removed" is a feature you do not actually need, a traditional architecture is cheaper and faster. tester checklist verify the front end never holds keys: signing happens in the wallet, and the dapp only sees the signed payload. test gas/revert paths: confirm the ui reflects a failed transaction state rollback instead of silently reporting success. validate access control: call admin only contract functions as a non admin and confirm they revert. check reentrancy and integer overflow behavior with a dedicated security review — these are the classic exploit classes. confirm the ui reads fresh state post confirmation and does not show optimistic stale balances. exercise multi sig wallets: a transaction authorized by fewer than the quorum must remain pending and unexecutable. verify wallet disconnect/revocation: permissions granted to the dapp are revocable and never persist keys. related architecture planning  what this system is the owasp top 10 2021 is the de facto checklist of the most common and dangerous web app weaknesses. it is about implementation risk — the flaws that live in code — and complements design level methods like stride and zero trust. the ten, distilled category one line meaning classic fix a01 broken access control users reach things they shouldn't enforce authz server side, deny by default, no client side role fields a02 cryptographic failures crypto used wrong or not at all tls everywhere, strong kdfs, no homemade crypto, encrypted secrets at rest a03 injection untrusted data executed as code parameterized queries, strict validation, output encoding a04 insecure design the design has no guardrails threat model first, deny by default, rate limits, architecture review a05 misconfiguration defaults, debug modes, open buckets config pipelines, security defaults, disable verbose errors a06 vulnerable components old libs with known cves dependency scanning, keep inventory, patch in sla a07 id & auth failures weak login, session bugs mfa, strong password rules, lockout, secure session handling a08 integrity failures deserialization, tampered pipelines signed artifacts, safe deserialization, check integrity a09 logging & monitoring gaps attacks invisible until too late structured + complete logs, alerting on anomalies, audit trails a10 ssrf server fetches attacker chosen urls validate allow listed destinations, no redirects to internal, metering how the "top" is decided the list is data driven : it re ranks by frequency in real scanners, reported incidents, and ctfs — not by academic severity. that's why design and ssrf entered in 2021: they were causing outsized damage even at low frequency. use it as a gate , not a scoreboard per feature : at design/implementation, test against the relevant rows an auth endpoint → a01/a07/a02; an import/export → a08/a03 . in ci : run the automated scans behind it zap, semgrep wired to fail the pipeline on hard/high findings. interviews & reviews : if you can't explain how you control a01, you have a gap — the top 10 makes that conversational and specific. it is not a threat model — it won't tell you a specific system's risk profile; pair it with stride for design and zero trust for enforcement. tester checklist map your app's endpoints against all ten and note which tax at least one control per applicable row. confirm access control is server enforced: stripping client side flags must break elevated features. enable verbose errors in staging and confirm the staging baseline is not your production config. spot check the dependency scanner: known cve packages must block the build, not just warn. related systems security standards landscape ../security ../zero trust architecture — the enforcement layer behind these controls authentication vs authorization ../authentication vs authorization — a01/a07 in action17:T982,threat modeling with stride a repeatable way to find design flaws before they become exploits — diagram your system, enumerate threats with stride, then rank and mitigate. security-architect solution-architect tester security architecture planning  what this system is threat modeling is not a pentest — it happens before code or before major changes by asking "if someone attacks us, which of these six generic threats would bite?" stride names the six: letter threat the attacker… example s spoofing pretends to be someone else forging a username / token t tampering modifies data or code changing a transfer amount mid flight r repudiation denies doing something "i never placed that order" no audit trail i information disclosure reads data not meant for them viewing another user's dashboard d denial of service makes the system unusable flooding an endpoint e elevation of privilege gains rights they lack turning a reader into an admin how to run a five minute pass 1. draw it : components, data stores, trust boundaries the dashed lines where identity flips — ui vs api vs db . 2. for each boundary, ask stride : at the crossing from the internet → app, s and i and d dominate; at app → db, t and i dominate. 3. score 1–3 each : likelihood × impact. fix anything ≥ 6 first — don't gold plate the rest. 4. prove mitigations : tests that would fail if a threat returned see checklist . common pitfalls modeling threats you can't ship — a mitigation that never ships is worse than none "documented as risk" ≠ fixed . forgetting the insider — the most expensive threats often sit inside trust boundaries shared db access . owasp ≠ stride — owasp top 10 is implementation bugs; stride is design. both belong on the roadmap, at different stages. tester checklist choose the top ranked threat and write a test that would have caught it. verify mitigations exist at the right trust boundary, not one hop downstream. confirm repudiation is covered: audit logs include actor, action, timestamp, and are append only. re run the dfd when the design changes — models rot fast. related systems zero trust architecture ../zero trust architecture — stride finds what zero trust then enforces session cookies and csrf ../session cookies and csrf — a classic stride finding spoofing via cookie / csrf noise api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — draw the dfd of the service you're threat modelling18:Tad1,togaf and the adm the architecture development method: a repeatable 8-phase cycle for moving from architecture vision to implemented, governed change. enterprise-architect solution-architect architecture enterprise-architecture planning  what this framework is togaf the open group architecture framework answers the question zachman deliberately avoids: how do you actually produce architecture? its engine is the adm — architecture development method — a staged cycle from vision to overshoot to closure control. what each phase produces phase output your organisation can use typical audience question preliminary principles, tooling choices, architecture repo "how will we even keep this?" a · vision stakeholder map, value proposition, high level roadmap "why are we doing this?" b · business capability map, value streams, target kpis "which processes improve?" c · information systems data + application architecture see zachman what/how "which systems and data change?" d · technology technology reference architecture, platform choices "which servers, cloud, tooling?" e · opportunities work packages, project portfolio "what do we build first?" f · migration transition architectures + a sequenced migration plan "how do we get there safely?" g · governance architecture compliance reviews, change control "is the build still on spec?" h · change architecture change requests, continuous refinement "what happens when reality disagrees?" the one thing that makes it "togaf" the adm is iterative and requirement driven , not a one shot waterfall. every arrow from b→h is hybrid with the requirements management loop in the middle: two traps kill adm rollouts in practice: 1. phases a–d done with no e–g — architecture becomes a shelf document nobody implements. togaf is explicit: opportunities, migration, and governance are part of the cycle, not afterthoughts. 2. method without a repository — without an architecture repository the "meta model" that stores artefacts , every new proposal regenerates the same diagrams by hand. how a solution architect works the cycle tester friendly checklist traceability: every phase c requirement must map to an adm artefact and back — a governance review will ask for it. baseline vs target diffs: the transition architecture phase f is what gets tested first, before the target exists. compliance reviews phase g need to run during implementation, not at go live. related systems the zachman framework ../zachman framework — the classification grid togaf fills cell by cell api the repo19:Tb3e,zero trust architecture never trust, always verify — identity-aware, micro-segmented networks that assume breach instead of defending a perimeter. security-architect enterprise-architect solution-architect security architecture enterprise-architecture zero-trust  what this system is classic network security is a castle : walls firewall around an internal "trusted" lan, where anything inside is assumed safe. zero trust inverts that assumption — every request, from anywhere, is untrusted until it proves identity, context, and policy. the three pillars 1. assume breach — design as if the attacker is already inside the network. lateral movement is the target to stop. 2. identity is the new perimeter — access is granted per user + device + context, not per subnet. mutual tls mtls authenticates both sides. 3. enforce every request — no "trusted once, trusted forever": re evaluate on each session, each request. key building blocks block role identity provider sso source of user identity and posture signals policy engine pep/pdp decides every allow/deny per request + context mtls service mesh encrypts + authenticates between services , not just at the edge microsegmentation logical isolates per workload so a compromise can't hop services just in time access privileged access granted for a window, then revoked a request under zero trust why teams adopt it lateral movement dies : even with vpn access, a user only reaches what policy allows per workload. blast radius shrinks : a stolen workstation does not equal "everything". remote anywhere becomes safe : zero network sprawl, zero "just add my home ip". costs are real : identity sprawl, per service policies, and latency from continuous checks — budget for them. tester checklist verify policy is evaluated on every request, not cached at login. confirm mtls actually fails when a service is impersonated. check that a device with a revoked posture signal loses access promptly not at next login . look for bootstrap holes: is there a path that trusts "internal network" implicitly? related systems authentication vs authorization ../authentication vs authorization — the per request authz zero trust depends on oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — identity layer every zt design plugs into togaf and the adm ../togaf framework — where a zero trust target architecture lives in the adm cycle deep dives identity and access management ../identity and access management — the identity layer the whole model rests on conditional access and policy engines ../authentication vs authorization — work profile identity gate for corporate apps1b:T8ad,api gateway caching cache-control headers, stale-while-revalidate, and cache keys — use the interactive diagram to poke at where a response can be served from. developer backend frontend tester api performance architecture  what this system is a cache at the edge cdn or gateway is the biggest latency win available: a hit never touches your origin. this guide focuses on how cache entries are decided and invalidated — the interactive diagram below is the same flow you can drag around. the cache decision, in plain words 1. the gateway computes a cache key — typically method + path + normalized query + auth scope . 2. it looks the key up in the edge cache. 3. hit : serve directly. miss : forward to origin. 4. the origin answers with a cache control header that says how long it may be cached and by whom . 5. on the first request after expiry, the gateway can serve the stale copy while fetching fresh data in the background = stale while revalidate . headers that matter header meaning cache control: max age=60 cache for 60 s cache control: public shared caches may store it cache control: private only the browser may store it — often right for user specific data etag validator: revalidate with if none match for a cheap 304 vary: accept encoding cache key must include the language/encoding of the response the classic bugs a private only response cached publicly → user a sees user b's dashboard. ignoring the cache key for auth → authenticated and anonymous users sharing entries. unbounded max age on mutable data → broken or stale product data until expiry. missing vary → a caches compressed and uncompressed variants under the same key. tester checklist inspect the cache control header on responses — is private data marked private? change a user's profile and confirm the edge does not still serve the old version. with stale while revalidate , confirm the first post expiry request is still fast stale served . check age header to see how old a cached response actually is. related systems api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — the chain this cache sits inside oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — why authenticated responses need careful cache keys1c:T728,authentication vs authorization the two questions every request must answer — who are you, and what are you allowed to do — and why conflating them is a classic breach. developer backend tester security-architect security api architecture  what this system is authentication authn proves who you are. authorization authz decides what you may do . they are separate stages with separate mechanisms — and the fastest way to leak data is to check one and assume the other happened. the sequence, spelled out watch the failure modes mistake consequence skip authz after authn any logged in user reaches admin endpoints judge by isauthenticated flag only no per resource boundaries authorize with data the client claims ?role=admin tampering grants privileges same check for read vs write a read only user can mutate state empty /403 on wrong role leaks that the resource exists common pairings concept where it lives authn: passwords, mfa, oauth tokens identity provider, login flow authz: rbac, abac, scopes, acls policy engine, middleware, per resource guards authn failure 401 unauthorized authz failure 403 forbidden a shorthand many architects use: authn tells you the who , authz tells you the what — never skip the second question. tester checklist verify each endpoint's authz independently of authn — log in as a low privilege user and hit privileged endpoints. check 403 arrives without revealing whether the resource exists. test that scopes / roles are read from the server token/db , never from a client supplied field. related systems oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the authn handshake for tokens jwt access tokens ../jwt compute1e:T11db,azure api management (apim) apim's anatomy — gateway, publisher portal, developer portal, subscriptions, policies, and identity — and the request path every request takes through it. developer backend solution-architect security-architect tester api cloud azure threat-modeling architecture  what this system is azure api management apim is a managed front of house for apis: every call to your backend services passes through its gateway, which enforces keys, applies transformation policies, meters usage, and can mock or fail safely. it's the azure native answer to "put something in front of our apis that isn't a bespoke gateway". three surfaces matter: the gateway runtime , the publisher portal you/control plane , and the developer portal your consumers discover and try your apis . the request path every request runs through the policy pipeline twice: once inbound auth, limit, transform before hitting your backend and once outbound normalize responses, strip internal headers, add caching . that's apim's whole superpower: behavior is added between consumer and backend without touching backend code. tiers and where to put it tier positioning fits developer testing, low sla, no sla uptime guarantee dev/qa only basic entry production, cheaper, limited features small apis, low traffic standard production default, sla, more policies most production workloads premium vnet injection, multi region, 10k calls/sec enterprise, regulatory, dr apim is typically deployed in front of your other paas functions, app service, aks, logic apps — acting as the single entry point, so those backends can be locked down to accept traffic only from the gateway. putting it behind your own network premium + vnet is how apim becomes an internal api router too. identity: keys and oauth the three layers of apim identity most teams need: subscription keys prove who's calling which registered app , used for metering/quotas — but they are not security: they travel in plaintext headers and are shared. never rely on them as an authz boundary. validate jwt policy checks the jwt a caller presents: issuer, audience, expiry, and claims. this is where your identity provider's tokens actually gate access. client certificates / mtls for machine to machine when keys are too weak — apim can require the caller's cert on the connection. the policy dsl policies are declarative xml that read like no code middleware: the patterns that matter in production: rate limit & quota protect the backend , validate jwt identity , set backend service route to a different backend per environment , mock return canned responses so the frontend can build while the backend catches up , and cors or better, keep cors off and front it properly . key gotchas keys ≠ authz. treat subscription keys as throttling/metering, never as a security perimeter. the gateway adds latency — a few ms, but every request pays it; benchmark with apim in and out of the path. default response caching just caches : set proper cache control and policy cache store to benefit, or you cache stale mutable data. don't let every backend call route through apim for high throughput internal chatter — use it as the front door , not the plumbing. policy errors silently 500 unless you add error handling: put retry , on error sections so transient backend failures have a story. tester checklist auth matrix : no key, wrong key, expired/malformed jwt, wrong audience — verify each gets the intended status 401/403/429 , not a leak to the backend. rate limit : burst past the limit and confirm 429s + headers; confirm quota resets on the defined window. transform fidelity : a header transform policy on outbound actually strips internal headers server, internal ips . backend management ../identity and access management — entra id is the identity layer this rides on20:T15c5,azure integration services service bus, event grid, logic apps, event hubs, and the api management front door — the toolkit that connects azure workloads to each other and to the rest of the enterprise. solution-architect enterprise-architect backend devops-engineer integration cloud azure architecture event-driven  what this system is azure's integration story is a toolkit, not a product: service bus enterprise messaging , event grid event routing between azure services , event hubs high throughput stream ingestion , logic apps no/low code orchestration glue , plus api management as the front door. there is no "right" single service — each solves a different delivery semantics problem, and real systems use several at once. the four services, and when each wins service delivery model best for service bus ordered queues + topics, at least once, ttl, dlq enterprise work queues, durable decoupling, competing consumers event grid pulses events to subscribers, auto retry, dead letter connecting azure resources "blob created → trigger function" , fan out event hubs massive throughput, partition keyed, no per message ack telemetry, click streams, anything with very high event volume logic apps orchestration engine low code workflows across systems, human approvals, no code glue the selector rule: a system that must process a message reliably later = service bus; an event azure itself should react to = event grid; a firehose of telemetry = event hubs; a multi step workflow = logic apps. they compose — it's common to receive on service bus, route decisions through logic apps, and publish results onto event grid. service bus: the durable work horse queues & topics : a queue has one logical consumer pool competing consumers share work ; a topic fans out to many subscriptions, each with its own durable copy. locking gives exactly once ish processing : a consumer gets an exclusive lock, must complete within the lock duration, or the message is retried — never duplicated in flight, but at least once overall, so handlers stay idempotent. settle one way : complete done , abandon redeliver , defer park to peek later , or dead letter for poison messages — the dlq is where bad payloads go instead of poisoning the queue forever. ordering caveat : queues/topics give ordering only per the session /partition key you set; safest for meaningful order is a sessionful queue where each session id is processed serially. event grid: the reactive router the defining trait of event grid: it routes, not stores . events are small facts "a blob appeared" , delivered to matching subscribers with retries and a dead letter sink, but there's no per message audit log like service bus. it's the fastest path between azure resources — trivial to wire blobcreated → functions with no code in the middle. event hubs: the firehose event hubs is a streaming ingestion bus : it mirrors kafka's partition/offset model so that high volume producers land events quickly and consumers functions, spark, aks read at their own pace. order is guaranteed per partitionkey . it is not for workload queues no per message ack like service bus and not for routing that's event grid — it's the place before processing, where millions of events/sec arrive and get persisted briefly. logic apps: orchestration without app code logic apps is control flow, not compute : it sequences steps calls, approvals, transforms , persists state, handles retries, and shows you the whole workflow visually. when a process is "send invoice → get finance approval → update erp → email the submitter", that's a logic app — because encoding it in a service would be glue, not value. putting it together: a reference shape for most integration first estates the shape is remarkably consistent: apim at the front, service bus for durable work, functions as workers, event grid to fan results out, event hubs only when there's a true firehose, logic apps for the human step workflows. tester checklist delivery semantics drill : kill a consumer mid message; verify the message is redelivered at least once and that the handler's idempotency dedupes the duplicate. poison message : send a bad payload repeatedly; confirm it dead letters, and that the dlq is inspected and drained on a schedule. session/order : with a sessionful queue, insert out of order and verify each session id processes serially. fan out : publish one topic message; confirm every subscription received its own durable copy. event grid retry : fail a subscriber endpoint and verify retries + dead letter sink, and check deliverycount /diagnostics logs. lock expiry : set a short lock, process slowly, and confirm complete on an expired lock surfaces as a duplicate/retry the consumer must tolerate. related systems api gateway caching ../api gateway caching and azure api management apim boot, rails, and django: routing, middleware, dependency injection, and sync vs async. developer backend tester web backend api  what this system is a backend framework turns "here is an http request" into "here is a behaved response" in a predictable way: middleware runs first, a router picks a handler, the controller/service does the work, and the response goes back out — with error handling, auth, and logging as layers in between. a request through the framework framework vs library library express, fastify, flask framework nestjs, spring boot, rails, django, asp.net core who calls whom your code calls it per route it provides a lifecycle; it calls you structure you decide folders/patterns conventions + scaffolding baked in boilerplate minimal at first more, but consistent across teams the trade off freedom, drift between devs discipline, harder to bend the classic first project algorithm: few endpoints + a small team → library; a product with many engineers → framework — so the architecture isn't reinvented per feature. the framework map framework language shape stands out for express / fastify node.js minimal, middleware apis, microservices, start ups of any size nestjs node.js ts opinionated, di modules structure, decorators, testability fastapi python async, type driven fast iteration, auto openapi docs django / rails python / ruby batteries, mvc, admin crud products fast; conventions spring boot java/kotlin heavyweight, enterprise large orgs, strong typing, di asp.net core c high perf, structured windows orgs, high throughput sync vs async where the bugs live thread per request spring/rails with blocking db drivers : each request holds a thread. fine until your pool fills under a slow db — then requests queue. event loop/async node, python asyncio , kotlin coroutines : few threads multiplex many requests. great for io bound work; dangerous if a handler blocks the loop with cpu work or a sync call. the bug that bites either way: blocking the wrong model — a cpu loop on an async loop, or seconds long work on a single thread pool. tester checklist verify the full pipeline fires for one request: middleware ran in order, route matched, service called once, response shaped right inspect logs/body . confirm a middleware abort short circuits the whole chain auth rejection must not reach the controller or db . test error handling: an exception in the service must produce the framework's standardized error body, not a raw stack trace. probe the blocking failure: hammer an endpoint that calls a slow service and confirm throughput degrades predictably that's the async/latency signature . related systems api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — the end to end path this framework lives inside frontend frameworks and rendering queue delivery ../message queue delivery — offloading work the request can't wait for22:T1419,banking risk, fraud, and aml controls the control layer wrapped around every bank transaction: credit and market risk, real-time fraud detection, kyc/aml screening, and regulatory reporting. security-architect solution-architect data-architect enterprise-architect tester banking risk fraud aml compliance security  what this system is banks must not only transact — they must know their customer is who they claim, their money is not laundered, and their losses are survivable . that is the risk and compliance layer: a set of checks that sit alongside the core ledger ../core banking systems and payment rails ../payments clearing and settlement , screening risk, fraud, and money laundering on every transaction, and reporting the results to regulators. it is genuinely a second system behind the account ledger: while account balance is the primary record, the risk posture of a customer, a transaction, and the bank itself is what gets examined under audit. the three control layers control question it answers latency required credit & market risk is the exposure, the counterparty, or the market move beyond appetite? near real time, plus batch fraud detection is this the rightful owner making this transaction from this device/path? milliseconds real time aml / kyc is this customer/sender/beneficiary sanctioned, pep, or patterned like laundering? seconds on transaction, broader on onboarding the trap is mixing them: a credit decision "can we afford this counterparty" is different from a fraud decision "is this really the customer" , and conning them together either rejects good business or lets bad through. architects should keep the data model one funnel with several scorers , not one scary "risk flag". real time fraud, step by step the design tension is customer friction vs false positives . a step up challenge otp, biometric, or re login stops fraud cheaply and is reversible; a hard decline is not. good fraud systems are calibrated to push uncertainty toward step up rather than blanket decline, and the calibration is a business decision owned jointly with the risk team. kyc and aml: the know your customer loop onboarding : identity verification document + liveness , pep/sanctions screening, and risk rating at account opening. ongoing monitoring : transaction monitoring looks for structuring, rapid movement, and inconsistent profiles; sanctions lists are refreshed continuously. reporting : suspicions become str/sar suspicious transaction/activity reports — a regulator required, confidential filing that must not tip off the customer. the net effect : an aml program is a filter that produces regulated outputs , so the boring engineering — reliable feeds, dedupe, auditable history, and case management — matters more than any single scorer. risk appetite: the bank's own threshold risk controls are only meaningful against an explicit appetite: limits per customer, per sector, per counterparty, and per product. the architecture must let treasury/credit set limits in one place and have every channel and every payment rail read them — the same refusal should show in the api, the branch, and the wire room. disjointed limit tables are how a bank discovers its exposure was really double what any one system reported. tester checklist fraud threshold: craft the same transaction from warm, stepped up, and anomalous contexts — each routes to the intended outcome auto pass, step up, block . friction rule: a medium risk event triggers step up exactly once per session, not a retry loop that abandons the customer. aml screening: a counterparty on a sanctions list is blocked at onboarding and at payment time, and the block generates the required case record. limited exposure: a customer limit raised in treasury is reflected in the wire, card, and api paths within the sla, not per system lazily. str handling: a suspicious transaction produces a report that is fully audited and never leaks to the customer facing view. idempotency of monitoring: replays of a transaction feed do not double count the monitoring signal or generate duplicate cases. calibration evidence: every pass/step up/deny decision can be re derived from its features for the regulator, with an auditable model version. related systems core certificates and pki — the cryptographic custody protecting the control layer23:T13e4,business process modeling and management from process maps to running workflows: bpmn notation, process decomposition, the process lifecycle — model, automate, measure, improve — and where a process ends and a software system begins. enterprise-architect solution-architect backend bpm workflow process architecture  what this system is a business process is the sequence of activities, decisions, and hand offs that turns an input into an outcome the business cares about — a lead becomes a signed order , a goods receipt becomes a payment . business process management bpm is the discipline of making those processes visible, measured, and improvable : you cannot improve a process you cannot see. a process is not the software that supports it. the process is the shared understanding of who does what, when, and what if ; the software a crm, an erp, an approval engine is one possible mechanism for carrying it out. when the process and the system disagree, you have a live process bug. the anatomy of a process every process can be decomposed into a few load bearing elements: element question it answers failure mode activity what gets done? activity with no named owner event what starts/stops it? process waiting on an event nobody publishes decision which branch do we take? rules hidden in someone's head actor / role who does it? hand off to a role that has no inbox sla / deadline how long is acceptable? deadline with no escalation data / artifact what passes between steps? "we always knew it" — but no system knows it the flow is the skeleton; the decision rules are the organs; the escalation is the immune system. a process modeled without deadlines and escalations is a diagram, not a process. process decomposition decomposition answers a practical question: who owns which level? executives own the value chain, process owners own level 2, teams own tasks, and system owners own the automated steps. the classic failure is modeling everything at one level — too abstract for the implementer, too granular for the decision maker. the process lifecycle 1. model — draw the current as is process in bpmn, with swimlanes per role and explicit decisions. if a step has no swimlane, it has no owner. 2. automate or enable — decide what the system does rules, approvals, notifications versus what a human must still judge. over automating a human judgment step is how you build a bad workflow. 3. run — the process executes; every step leaves a process record who did it, when, how long so the process is audit able. 4. measure — kpis: cycle time, hand off count, first pass yield, wait time between steps. the process record is the source of the kpi. 5. improve — the measured pain points get redesigned; the new process is modeled, automated, and run again. where the process meets the system the integration pattern to teach: the business event starts the process, the rules decide the route, the system automates the routine steps, the humans handle the judgment, and every step lands in a process record. the record is what turns "we think it works" into "we measured it." business process model and notation bpmn bpmn is the drawing language of processes: events circles , activities rounded rectangles , gateways diamonds , swimlanes who does what . its superpower is precision — a bpmn diagram can be so unambiguous that an engine can execute it directly bpmn is both diagram and code . the pitfalls are the same as any modeling: model decisions as gateways with named conditions , not as arrows with vibes. model exceptions explicitly — "what if the vendor rejects?" is a normal path, not an edge case. keep the diagram executable or explainable : if an engine would get stuck, a human will too. tester checklist swimlane audit: walk the as is process and confirm every activity sits in a named role's lane with a named owner. exception drill: run the process with the rejection / error path vendor rejects, customer cancels and confirm the exception route exists and escalates. kpi provenance: pick one measured kpi and trace it back to specific fields in the process record — it must be computable, not asserted. decision honesty: check that every gateway condition is explicit enough that a new analyst and the workflow engine would both pick the same branch. hand off latency: measure wait time between two swimlanes — the place most processes actually lose their time. related systems software development lifecycle sdlc ../software development lifecycle — where process requirements become software, phase by phase system acquisition lifecycle ../system acquisition lifecycle — the build vs buy gate that decides whether sdlc or procurement follows erp systems ../enterprise resource planning — the system that carries many core processes order to cash, procure to pay integration patterns ../integration patterns — how the events that start and advance processes move between systems itil change and release management ../itil change and release management — the change process that governs process software, itself a process24:Ta77,change data capture (cdc) publishing your database writes as events without touching application code — reading the transaction log and streaming every insert, update, and delete. backend solution-architect tester integration database architecture  what this system is change data capture cdc turns the database's own write ahead log into an event stream. instead of the application remembering to emit events on every write and getting it wrong when it forgets , a cdc connector reads the binary log / wal and publishes each committed change. debezium is the reference open source implementation; every cloud vendor has a managed cousin. how a change becomes an event a single kafka connect pipeline can fan out one database change to a search index, a cache, a warehouse, and downstream systems — no dual write, no missing events, no application level publish. outbox vs cdc dual write app emits event outbox pattern cdc how events get created app publishes after write app writes event row in the same db transaction; relay publishes connector reads the db log consistency risk event and write can split crash between atomic — safe atomic — safe requires app changes requires it at every write one insert per event none catalog of changes only what you chose to emit only what you chose to emit everything , including schema events best for thin events, custom semantics reliable business events bulk sync, search/cache/analytics targets where cdc hurts schema changes become events alter table is in the log — consumers wired to the old shape break until they migrate. initial snapshot + continuous mode is where bugs hide: the boundary between "backfill everything" and "only delts" is easy to get wrong miss rows, or duplicate . order is per table by pk in the log ; deleting is represented by a "before, no after" tombstone. ddl heavy or high write databases produce enormous logs; you are paying to stream every write, even ones nobody consumes. tester checklist restart the connector mid stream and confirm exactly to exactly connection no missing, no duplicated rows via lsn/offset replay. suspend the source db for hours — confirm lag is bounded and recoverable rather than data loss. verify tombstones hard deletes propagate as deletes to the target, not as stale copies. test alter table exposure: consumers must tolerate or visibly fail, not silently corrupt. related systems pub/sub and event streaming ../pub sub event streaming — where cdc's events land message queue delivery ../message queue delivery — delivery semantics the downstream queue enforces integration patterns ../integration patterns — where cdc sits in the integration landscape25:Td1f,ci/cd pipelines from commit to production with gates: stages, artifact promotion, environments, and the deployment strategies that make releases boring. devops-engineer developer tester devops deployment workflow  what this system is ci continuous integration runs automated checks on every commit — lint, test, build — and the cd continuous delivery/deployment half packages the result into an artifact and promotes it through environments until it reaches production. the output is not "a deploy"; it's a repeatable, auditable path. the stages that never change even when tools do stage purpose typical checks commit/pr fast feedback on the change lint, unit tests, type checks build encapsulate the app as one artifact build image, sbom test prove it behaves integration, e2e, security scan package/promote make the artifact official book that exact sha, not "latest" deploy staging safe rehearsal env parity, smoke tests deploy prod the real thing, controlled way canary/blue green, rollback trigger the rule to hold onto: you promote build artifacts, never recompile . if prod rebuilds from source, you're not releasing the tested thing. environment as a "gate" environments are promotion steps , not copies: staging should run on the same config shape and secrets as production, or the gate proves nothing. deployment strategies compared strategy how it releases downtime risk profile recreate stop old, start new downtime simplest, riskiest for live rolling replace nodes gradually none single version alive at any moment, mixed briefly blue green new stack beside old, switch traffic none a cutover instant rollback by switching back canary send small % to new, increase if healthy none catch problems on real traffic, earlier feature flag release ship code dark, toggle in prod none decouple deploy from release the mature answer is usually canary or blue green for infra changes, plus feature flags for product changes — "the code is in prod" and "users see it" become two different events. what makes a pipeline trustworthy security too secrets only via the pipeline's secret store — never in the build step's logs or schema files. signed/sbommed artifacts : pin checksums, so prod deploys exactly the tested bytes. every deploy is a git commit + tag : you can name who/what/when by looking at the repo, not guessing. manual gates where context matters, automation elsewhere : let humans click for prod, machines do the rest. tester checklist corrupt a build artifact and confirm promotion blocks it integrity fails at staging, never a prod surprise . verify staging truly mirrors prod: same secret keys, same env vars, same region/scale shape. trigger an alarm on the canary wave and confirm rollback fires to the previous artifact within your mttr. prove any change to the pipeline definition is itself reviewed and versioned in git. related systems git and version control ../git and version control — the commits and prs that trigger all of this devops delivery loop ../devops delivery loop — the wider loop this pipeline sits inside jira and agile delivery ../jira and agile delivery — where the work tickets for each stage live aws core services ../aws core services — where many pipelines land ecr, codebuild, pipeline dockerizing applications ../dockerizing applications — the image that pipelines build, scan, and promote26:Tc05,cloud computing fundamentals regions, availability zones, service models, the shared responsibility model, and designing for durability without writing a line of purchased iron. cloud-engineer enterprise-architect solution-architect cloud architecture enterprise-architecture  what this system is the cloud is a managed utility: you rent compute, storage, and network at region scale instead of owning data centers. the core mental model — regions + availability zones azs + service models — is identical across aws and azure even when the names differ. a region is a geographic cluster of 2 4+ availability zones — independent power, cooling, and fibre. a true cloud deployment tolerates losing one whole az and keeps serving. that is the single biggest resilience idea in cloud. the three service models iaas paas saas you manage app + some config app nothing but usage aws ec2, vpc rds, lambda, ecs s3? no — saas is like office 365 azure vms, vnet app service, functions saas skus you consume best for legacy lift, full control ship apps fast, minimal ops just use it your job as a cloud engineer is mostly choosing the model per workload : databases on iaas means patching mysql yourself; on paas you get patching, backups, and failover for free — and pay accordingly. shared responsibility model the boundary moves depending on the service: with iaas you own os to top; with paas/saas the provider owns almost everything below your code — but you always own your data, your iam, and your key material. "the cloud encrypted it" is never a substitute for your own encryption decisions. designing for failure three numbers shape every design: rpo how much data you can lose and rto how fast you must be back and cost what you'll pay for both . multi az + automated failover shrinks rpo/rto; backups are the safety net when both fail. cost levers the art of not overspending right size then autoscale : most cloud bill statements are runaway idle vms. reserved/commitment plans for steady load, spot for batch/fault tolerant work. storage tiers : hot/cold/archive — put the audit logs in archive, not hot s3/blob. serverless lambda/functions is often the cheapest for spiky workloads and the most expensive for sustained steady load. tester checklist kill/stop one az's instances in a staging account and verify the app re serves from the remaining azs within your rto. restore from a backup into a scratch region and confirm rpo is at most your stated window. verify iam/rbac is least privilege: no cloud admin on daily users, no giant allow on read buckets. watch a month of cost report: which two line items most exceed the forecast? right size them. related systems aws core services ../aws core services — the same fundamentals with aws names azure core services ../azure core services — the same fundamentals with azure names cloud networking and hybrid connectivity ../cloud networking and hybrid connectivity — how regions talk to each other and your office zero trust architecture ../zero trust architecture — cloud identity and posture at scale27:Tb86,cloud landing zones the foundation vpc/vnet peering, transit hubs, private endpoints, and the vpn/direct-connect/expressroute exits that tie the cloud to your office. cloud-engineer network-engineer solution-architect cloud network architecture  what this system is cloud networks are software. a vpc aws or vnet azure defines an isolated ip space; you split it into subnets, decide what goes public vs private, connect regions by peering/transit , and reach your office through vpn or private dedicated links . the skill is trading connectivity against exposure. how vpcs talk to each other and to you way to connect vpc/vnet what it gives watch outs peering two networks, direct private ip no transitive hops peering is point to point vpc transit gateway / hub a hub routes to many spokes hub becomes a spof rightsize + protect it private endpoint / vpc endpoint reach a service by private ip without traversing the internet endpoint per service; watch costs vpn gateway / tunnel site to site encrypted over internet best effort throughput, does not beat dedicated dedicated connect dx / expressroute private, stable, high throughput to provider slow to provision, dedicated cost rule of thumb: hub and spoke with a transit layer , private endpoints for sensitive services, and public internet only for what must be public. the add lecture: peerings don't cross regions silently and peering is not transitive — if you have many vpcs, use the hub, don't chain peerings. private endpoint vs "just a security group" a security group/nsg filtering a public facing db is still on the public internet — one mis rule and it leaks. a private endpoint takes the resource entirely off the public surface: only in network traffic can reach it. prefer endpoints for anything holding data. security basics that differ per platform aws trust architecture — reducing trust per subnet and endpoint, not per network29:T1412,cmdb and configuration management what a cmdb actually is — the cis, relationships, and attributes that model your estate — and why most fail for cultural reasons before technical ones. enterprise-architect solution-architect cloud-engineer devops-engineer itsm enterprise-architecture operations asset  what this system is a configuration management database cmdb is a database of configuration items cis — servers, applications, services, licenses, network devices, databases, people owned assets — plus the relationships between them, plus the attributes that matter owner, version, environment, patch status . it is not a fancy inventory: a spreadsheet of assets is a list, and a cmdb is a model . the difference is the relationship graph — "this application runs on that server which connects to that database" — because that graph is what incident, change, and dependency analysis all query. cis, relationships, attributes concept example why it matters ci web prod 01 , erp application v11 the atomic unit you reason about attribute owner, env, version, patch level what you filter and report on relationship app runs on server , server connects to db the graph that makes incidents diagnosable status installed, retired, in prod keeps the model honest about what's real the useful cmdb is the one where the relationship graph is current enough that "what breaks if this server dies?" and "who owns this ci?" get a trustworthy answer. that trust is the whole ballgame: a cmdb that nobody believes gets abandoned, and an abandoned cmdb is worse than none because it lies with authority. the trust cycle why cmdbs fail every cmdb project is a bet on which cycle you'll be on in a year. the technical half is easy discovery tools, adapters, a schema . the cultural half is decisive: if consuming a ci record costs less effort than guessing, the cmdb lives; if a stale record ever burns someone, it dies. the practices that keep the good cycle spinning: ownership as a first class attribute — every ci names a human; stale records get chased to their owner, not silently cleaned. discovery feeds the model automatically — cloud, vms, agents, network scans update cis without a human typing them. consumers close the loop — incident, change, and capacity teams query the cmdb as their default first move, which creates the feedback that keeps it honest. a ci is retired the day it's retired — the model must reflect reality or it decays from day one. cmdb vs asset management vs inventory store tracks the question it answers hardware/asset register purchased things, ownership, lifecycle, cost "what do we own and who's paying for it?" inventory/software list install counts, licenses "what software runs and are we compliant?" cmdb cis and their relationships "what runs on what, and who does it affect?" they share data and often live in the same tool, but their purpose differs: the asset register is about money and custody, the cmdb is about impact . "which users does this database support?" is a cmdb question; "how many macs do we have?" is an asset register question. conflating them is the most common reason a "cmdb implementation" ships an inventory that nobody consults during an incident. discovery and the source of truth the single source of truth question is where cmdb projects overreach. the healthy answer is federation with one reconciled layer : discovery tools feed each asset type, humans own the exceptions, and the cmdb publishes the agreed record to whoever consumes it. the unhealthy answer is "we will model every aws resource, vm, and network interface manually" — that's how a cmdb rots in a quarter. tester checklist the impact question : pick a database ci, ask "which applications and users would be affected if it failed" — confirm the relationship graph returns the right set. staleness drill : retire a server in reality, keep it in the cmdb, and confirm either discovery removes it or the owner gets paged within the sla you set. trust test : compare cmdb ci versions against reality agents/cloud api for a sample and measure drift — under a few percent is healthy, double digits is dying. ownership check : every ci in a sample must resolve to a named human owner, not "it". discovery fidelity : create a new vm in the cloud and time how long it takes to appear as a ci with the right relationships. pipelines how images become labels: ingestion, preprocessing, detection, classification, and the hardware and latency realities of production vision systems. developer backend tester solution-architect data-architect ai architecture data pipeline  what this system is computer vision turns pixels into decisions. a camera produces frames; your system must locate objects detection , describe them classification , track them across time tracking , and often do it fast and close to the source. the model is the star, but four things decide whether a vision pipeline survives production: data plumbing , preprocessing , model serving , and the hardware you run it on. ingestion: streams, not files frame rate vs value : 30 fps of raw video burns bandwidth and gpu for little gain. most pipelines sample — 1–5 fps for people counting, event triggered capture for alerts. stream middleware kafka, kinesis, mqtt buffers and fans frames out so a slow model never forces a dropped camera. sampled persistence : store all detections small json , store a fraction of full frames they're huge and rarely needed later . preprocessing: small, cheap, and consistent three rules that matter more than any model trick: match training preprocessing exactly — resize method, mean normalization, channel order bgr vs rgb all quietly destroy accuracy if mismatched. do it before the model, off the critical path — decode/resize with hardware acceleration gpu/dsp, or codecs , never in python loops. resize is a trade, not a detail — smaller input = faster, but small objects disappear. match input resolution to the smallest object you must detect. detection vs classification pick the cheapest tool that answers the question: classification answers "which one?" — a single image in, one label out. cheapest, fastest, best accuracy per compute. detection answers "where and what?" — boxes, needed when objects are among clutter or multiple. segmentation answers "exactly which pixels?" — expensive; use only when pixel level boundaries matter medical imaging, autonomous driving . model serving: the latency stack batch, don't stream one by one : gpus are throughput machines; group frames even 4–8 to amortize fixed overhead. serve next to the data : edge deployment jetson, edge gpu appliance in the factory/office beats round tripping frames to cloud — the latency and bandwidth math usually decides it: cloud for batch, edge for real time. post process off the model : nms non max suppression to collapse overlapping boxes, confidence thresholds, and class filtering run cheaply in cpu code — don't pay gpu for them. the hardware reality where it runs typical stack when on device / edge cpu + dsp/npu, int8 quantized model real time, privacy, offline on prem gpu tensorrt/optimized engine box sustained high fps, no cloud egress cloud gpu instances, auto scaled bursty, batch analysis, elastic cost quantization fp32 → int8 is the classic lever: 4× throughput for a small accuracy dip. combined with pruning and distillation it is how models the size of a small llm end up running on a $50 camera module. tester checklist data mismatch : feed preprocessed frames and raw frames to the same model assert accuracy is the same — preprocessing must not be the silent accuracy killer. corner cases : occluded objects, low light, mirrored images, and frames with the same object partially cut off at frame edges. determinism : two runs over identical inputs should produce identical outputs watch floating point variance across batch sizes . latency slo : measure end to end p95 under load, not the model's reported single inference time — queueing and batching dominate. drift : the live distribution lighting, angles, season drifts from training data; sample new detections periodically and compare confidence distributions. related systems llm agents and workflows ../llm agents and workflows — vision models feed a multimodal agent the same way rag feeds it per-request vs continuous evaluation, and just-in-time privilege. security-architect solution-architect enterprise-architect security identity zero-trust  what this system is a zero trust architecture executes decisions through a small engine: a policy enforcement point pep intercepts every request, and a policy decision point pdp answers allow, deny, or step up — by combining user, device, location, risk, and data signals on every request, not at login. the signals a pdp weighs signal what it proves can be spoofed? identity tokens + mfa "who" requires the credential — the core device posture endpoint compliance "from a healthy device" only if the endpoint is compromised location / ip / network "from where" yes — vpn, proxies anomaly / risk score velocity, geo, peer "behaves like the user" indirect — heuristic, not proof trusted app / capability "from a known appliance" the app's own auth the art is weighting : a risk signal alone never grants ; it only raises or lowers the bar. "logged in from an unusual country + new device + admin role" is not access — it's a step up prompt or a block. per request vs continuous evaluation per request : stateless checks on each call cheap, catches token/lacency changes . continuous/risk adaptive : background telemetry watches for changes device just got quarantined, token used from a second ip and downgrades live. where these go wrong: caching the decision at login "trusted once, trusted forever" — which is exactly the old perimeter habit zt is supposed to kill. just in time and privilege elevation privileged access is the crown jewel. jit / pam makes admin rights temporary and requested : a user asks, a grant appears for a window, then it evaporates. never "admin by default." the principle extends to service accounts too — short lived workload credentials workload identity ../workload identity and mtls , pki ../secrets certificates and pki are the machine analog. tester checklist trigger a live signal change mid session quarantine the device and confirm the next request is denied, not the next login. verify step up actually re authenticates: reaching a sensitive scope from an abnormal context requires mfa again. check that a risk only signal can never authorize — only block or escalate. time box a jit grant and confirm it expires on schedule with auditing of who requested it. related systems zero trust identity and mtls ../workload identity and mtls — policy for machine to machine calls too oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the token plumbing policies run on2c:T13f7,cpu and gpu, arm and x86 why cpus win on latency and gpus on throughput, why arm is eating the data center, and how to match the right silicon to the workload. cloud-engineer devops-engineer backend data-architect endpoint-device-engineer cloud architecture hardware performance  what this system is every compute decision comes down to two mismatches. cpu vs gpu : a cpu is a few very fast, generalist cores built for latency; a gpu is thousands of small, specialized cores built for throughput. arm vs x86 : two instruction set architectures isas that have converged in performance while diverging in economics. neither is "better" — each wins a different job, and choosing wrong is the most expensive mistake in cloud bills and ml latency. cpu vs gpu: latency vs throughput cpu is optimized for latency : complex control flow, big caches, deep pipelines, strong single thread performance. it runs your web server, your database queries, anything unpredictable. gpu is optimized for throughput : thousands of cores execute the same instruction on different data in lockstep simt . great where the same math runs on millions of points — graphics, matrix multiply, vector search, training batches. the gpu is not fast at one thing , it is fast at many at once . a single serial dependency chain runs slower on a gpu than on a cpu. the simt model in practice matrix a×b where both are 1024×1024 = 1 billion independent multiply adds. a cpu core does them mostly one at a time; a gpu spreads them across cores so the whole thing finishes in the time a cpu takes for a few thousand. that's the entire story of why ml training lives on gpus: the work is embarrassingly parallel, so throughput wins . but the same shape helps you find gpu bound workloads that aren't ml: image/video encoding, cryptographic hashing at scale, string/json processing, and vector search all parallelize. where gpus are a trap remember the classic exchange rate failure: data movement outweighs the math . copying 10 mb across pcie or network for cloud gpus can cost more than the compute would take on cpu. cheap workloads + expensive transfers = gpu accelerated slowness . arm vs x86: the isa split x86 intel, amd is cisc : complex instructions, massive backward compatibility, extremely strong single core. it remains the default for high clock latency bound work. arm is risc : simpler fixed width instructions, and — crucially — anyone can build one . apple m series , aws graviton , ampere, and qualcomm all ship arm because the isa license is buyable. what flipped arm from "phone chip" to "data center chip" is the perf per watt equation : graviton and m series deliver comparable throughput to x86 at a fraction of the power and cost. the real decision: perf per watt and total cost power density is now the ceiling. data centers sell watts, not cores. a chip that does the same work for half the power lets you run 2× the workload in the same rack — this is why cloud providers push arm instance families so hard. total cost math : arm instances e.g. aws graviton routinely come out 20 40% cheaper for the same throughput on scale out workloads — api servers, caches, web tiers, ci fleets. x86 still wins where latency is the sla and software is tied to x86 details : some commercial databases and legacy binaries ship x86 only, making the "cheaper" arm option a migration project. choosing compute shape by workload workload shape best silicon web api, gateway, app tier latency, spiky, serial arm scale out or x86 high clock database oltp latency, branchy x86 high clock / high cache; arm where tuned batch / etl / ci throughput, parallel arm many core or gpu for math heavy ml training parallel math gpu ml inference mix — batch = gpu, real time = cpu/edge gpu for batch, quantized on cpu/npu for edge vector search / encode parallel gpu or arm, measure legacy commercial software often x86 locked x86 tester checklist benchmark the real mix, not a microbenchmark : an arm vs x86 decision measured on one synthetically parallel test lies — run your actual request mix and total cost over a week. check power, not just speed : compare perf per watt, since rack power and cooling cap your actual scale. software lock in test : run your observability, commercial db, and security agents on the candidate arm image before committing — dwarves hide in middleware. gpu sanity check : profile your kernel's pcie transfer time vs compute time; if transfers dominate, the gpu is a liability. cost per result : for ml workloads, measure cost per 1k inferences/train epoch, not instance hour price. related systems cloud computing fundamentals ../cloud computing fundamentals — regions, azs, and the service model your chosen silicon runs as serverless computing and functions ../serverless computing and functions — where the platform hides the cpu/gpu decision from you computer vision pipelines ../computer vision pipelines — gpus and npus in real time inference systems and one agreed definition per metric: the catalog, access rules, and quality checks that turn a pile of data into something the business can trust and reuse. data-architect enterprise-architect security-architect solution-architect database architecture security  what this system is data governance is the answer to "whose data is this, what does it mean, who may use it, and is it true?" — enforced by a data catalog inventory + metadata , ownership every data set has a human answerable for it , access rules who may see what , and quality checks does the data match its claims . without it, every analytics team independently builds "the real revenue" and nobody agrees on what the number means. the four pillars pillar the question it answers what it looks like in practice catalog what data exists, where, and what does it mean? searchable inventory: tables, columns, descriptions, tags, owner ownership who is answerable for this data? a named owner/steward per data set, reviewable and on call for it access who may read/modify it, and why? role based permissions, pii classification, approval flows quality is it current, complete, and correct? freshness, row count, and validity checks with alerts ownership: one human per data set "governance" collapses into a single enforceable rule: every data set has one named owner , and that owner signs off on its definition, quality, and who may access it. the failure mode to avoid is an orphan data set — created three years ago by a team that left, trusted by nobody, cached everywhere. orphan data is how "the real number" starts having five versions. the catalog is the nervous system the catalog turns "where is the customer data?" from a tribal knowledge question into a search. lineage this table comes from orders via pipeline p1 , feeds report r2 is what makes impact assessment possible: "if we change this column, what breaks?" without lineage, every schema change is a roll of the dice against every report nobody can find. access and classification: the security spine classify first, gate second : mark pii personal data , sensitive, and public at the catalog level; access rules and masking attach to the classification, not to ad hoc roles. row/column level security for the sensitive columns: a consumer can read customers but not customers.email — masking at the catalog/engine layer, not "just don't select it." the least privilege audit : the recurring finding is a bi group with a warehouse wide "analyst" role that quietly reads pii they don't need. access follows a purpose , granted on request, reviewed. quality checks: the trust contract check catches freshness "last loaded 3 days ago" — the report is stale and nobody knows row count / volume pipeline silently stopped or partially loaded null/validity rate a column that's suddenly 80% null is a broken transform duplicate key check double counted fact rows definition agreement two dashboards disagreeing on the same metric one definition per metric is the governance hill to die on: revenue is either defined same sql, same grain, same timezone, certified in the catalog or it isn't a metric at all. the technical trick that makes this stick is metrics as governed artifacts a shared metrics/sql layer , not copied formula snippets in each dashboard. tester checklist pick a data set in the catalog and verify it has: an owner, a description, quality checks, and lineage to both its source and its consumers. if any are missing, that's the governance gap. try to access a pii column with a role that has no justification: it must be masked or denied, not simply "not mentioned." find two dashboards that show the "same" metric and confirm they read the same certified definition or surface the disagreement . kill a source pipeline and confirm the freshness monitor alerts within the sla — not after someone asks why the report is wrong. verify lineage impact: identify what downstream reports would break if a column is renamed, and confirm the catalog tells you that list before the change. related systems data warehouse and lakehouse architecture ../data warehouse lakehouse architecture — the systems governance
