is pii heavy and heavily governed44:T10b4,http authentication and basic auth how the authorization header actually works: basic auth's username:password encoding, www-authenticate challenges, digest, api keys, and bearer tokens — and when each is (or is not) safe. developer backend tester security-architect security api architecture  what this system is http authentication is the authorization request header plus the www authenticate response header that tells you how to authenticate . the header carries a scheme and scheme specific credentials: basic , bearer , digest , or a custom scheme. it is the oldest, simplest layer of "who may access this endpoint" — and the one most often misused. basic auth, honestly explained basic auth is one line: authorization: basic <base64 username:password . the credentials are base64 encoded, not encrypted — anyone who sees the header sees the password base64 is trivially reversible . three rules decide whether it's acceptable: 1. only over tls — over plain http, the header is the password broadcast to the network. 2. only for low stakes or system to system credentials — a shared api key with narrow scope, never a human's master password. 3. never against a database of user passwords — compare against a hash and rate limit to survive brute force . the classic failure: "it's behind https so base64 is fine" — then an operator pulls the header into a log, a proxy, or an error message, and the credential leaks in plaintext where the tls never protected it. the www authenticate dance when auth is missing/invalid, the server answers 401 with a www authenticate header naming the scheme and often a realm describing what's protected . the client then retries with credentials . that's why you'll see two requests in the network tab for a basic protected page. basic vs digest vs bearer vs api keys scheme how it works use it for danger basic base64 user:pass tls only, low stakes, simple credential in every header; replayable if leaked digest hash of user:pass + nonce legacy, still plaintext apps weak hash; rarely worth it today bearer an opaque token from an issuer tokens you already hold oauth/jwt whoever holds the token is you — no password involved api key a shared secret, often x api key system to system, scoped same leak problem as basic; also logged mtls certificate on the connection machine to machine, high trust complex to roll out; strongest tie to the wire bearer and api keys are the modern defaults for machine apis precisely because they don't carry a password — a leaked token is revocable and ideally short lived, whereas a leaked basic pair is the user's real login. the recurring refrain in every auth review: "this is a credential, treat it like one." reading the header: what a tester looks for is the header ever logged proxy, gateway, app logs ? one req.headers dumped to logs = credential leak. does the server check over the header only, or also caches a response keyed by the credential a cached 200 shared across users ? is there rate limiting / lockout on failures? a basic endpoint without it is a brute force treadmill. are credentials revocable and rotated ? a static api key from 2019 with access to prod is an incident waiting. tester checklist confirm every basic/bearer api you expose refuses plaintext http redirect to https, or reject entirely — the header must never travel unencrypted. attempt auth with the base64 decoded and sent wrong: expect 401 with www authenticate , not 200 or 403 with data. check that failed attempts are rate limited/locked and that logs do not contain the full authorization header. verify a leaked token scenario end to end: revoke a key/token and confirm in flight and new requests fail not just "stale cache" . distinguish 401 vs 403: unauthenticated = 401 + challenge; authenticated but forbidden = 403. mixing them up hides bugs and leaks resource existence. related systems oauth grant types ../oauth grant types — where bearer tokens come from instead of basic credentials oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the full token lifecycle basic has nothing to do with authentication vs authorization ../authentication vs authorization — authn this vs authz the next step jwt tokens ../jwt tokens — what a modern bearer token often contains45:Tcf2,identity and access management (iam) users and machines as first-class identities: directories, sso and federation, the identity lifecycle, and rbac vs abac. security-architect enterprise-architect backend identity security enterprise-architecture zero-trust  what this system is zero trust starts with identity. iam is the layer that answers permanently "who is this?" and "what may they do?" — for humans, service accounts, and workloads. if identity is wrong, every access decision built on top of it is wrong too. the four jobs of iam: prove sign up/sign in , attribute roles/groups/claims , issue tokens , and revoke when access must end . the sign in flow behind "one password" federation means the corporate idp owns the proof; each app trusts the same source. one revoke at the idp immediately kills access everywhere — the property every zt design depends on. lifecycle: where identity actually goes wrong phase what you do typical failure provision auto create account on hire, from hr feed manual, slow, delayed use groups from management/hr drive access groups drift, un reviewed change role change re scopes groups old rights linger to "not break anything" revoke offboard: disable idp + kill sessions + deprovision "disabled but still licensed"; orphan service accounts the orphaned account is the quiet disaster: a service account with admin rights nobody monitors. lifecycle automation and periodic access reviews are the counter measures. rbac vs abac the authorization models rbac roles abac attributes decides by "you are an admin" attributes: user, device, time, location, data tag pros simple, human readable granular, context sensitive cons role explosion as you grow policy sprawl if not disciplined fit small estates, clear job families zero trust / conditional access at scale most enterprises run both: rbac for coarse grants, abac/conditional access to narrow when they apply. tester checklist offboard a user: account disabled, sessions killed, and all app entitlements gone — verify actual revoke, not just "disabled". verify sso reaches every app: no app quietly keeps its own credential store shadow identity . replay an old jwt after revocation and confirm it's rejected short token ttl + revocation callback . walk a role change: new group grants, old group removed — then review that nothing re attaches via nested groups. related systems zero trust architecture ../zero trust joiner mover leaver jml ../joiner mover leaver — the lifecycle events that drive it all46:T172a,incident response playbooks the prepared scripts that turn 'something is wrong' into a calm, repeatable response — phases, roles, decision trees, and the post-incident loop that makes the next one faster. security-architect cloud-engineer devops-engineer tester security incident-response operations workflow  what this system is an incident response ir playbook is a prepared, rehearsed runbook for a specific class of bad day: ransomware, phishing, credential compromise, data breach, ddos, insider threat. its purpose is to remove thinking under panic — when the page fires, people execute a known script instead of improvising. unlike an alert or a ticket, a playbook is scenario shaped , has owners per step , and ends with a lessons learned loop . the ir team's real product is not "response" — it's the book of playbooks. the phase model whatever framework you use phase the question what happens typical owner preparation what will we do before? playbooks, tools, access, comms channels, contacts security leadership detection & triage is this real and how bad? verify the alert, assess scope, assign a severity/pager soc / on call containment how do we stop the spread now ? isolate hosts, kill access, revoke creds, cut egress ir team + network eradication what is the root cause to remove? remove malware, close the path, patch, change secrets security engineering recovery when is it safe to come back? restore from verified back ups, re enable services, validate service owners lessons learned what changes before the next one? root cause review, playbook edits, training, budgets the whole team the mental model: detect is cheap to perform and expensive to get wrong; contain is the hour that saves the estate. most of a playbook's value sits in triage who decides and containment the first concrete actions , because those run while everything is still on fire. a concrete playbook skeleton credential compromise good playbooks share a skeleton even when the scenario differs: 1. trigger conditions — exactly which alerts/tickets start this playbook. 2. initial actions 5–15 min — revoke, isolate, kill, reset: the steps that buy time. 3. decision gates — branching logic "if admin → different path" with named deciders. 4. escalation tree — who gets engaged at which severity, with a page tree not a guessing game. 5. evidence preservation — what to collect and where, before anyone "fixes" the evidence away. 6. verification + handoff — how everyone knows containment actually worked and who's running recovery. 7. retrospective hooks — where lessons feed back and the playbook gets edited for next time . why playbooks fail in the real world failure the real reason the fix written, not rehearsed a playbook nobody has ever run in a drill tabletop exercises and fire drills on a schedule no named owners "security team" is a meeting, not a person every step gets a named owner + a back up tools aren't provisioned the playbook says "revoke" but the revoke tool isn't accessible to on call dry run the access: can the on call person actually do the step? stale playbooks systems changed, playbook didn't version inside the cmdb ../cmdb and configuration management and re validate on infra change communications gap ransomware raced the playbook; nobody told the business a comms tree and template messages as part of the playbook recovery without verification service "restored" from backups that were also encrypted/laggy verified restores test the backup, not just believe the copy the deepest rule: a playbook is a living document, not a deliverable. if it's not edited after every incident and exercised between them, it's shelfware with a security branding. the post incident loop where teams actually improve the retrospective must separate blame from cause, then turn findings into three concrete edits : the playbook steps , the tooling access/automation gaps , and the training who wasn't ready . an incident response capability that doesn't measure time to contain across incidents isn't learning — it's just documenting. tester checklist trigger fidelity : raise the exact alert a playbook starts on and confirm the playbook's trigger conditions match reality no "we don't use that alert anymore" . 5 minute test : hand a fresh on call person the playbook and the tools, and time them to the first concrete containment action — it must be under the target, unaided. decision gate walk : simulate both branches of a gate user vs admin credential hit and confirm each path resolves with its named decider, not an abandoned thread. tool reality check : for each step claiming "revoke/reset/isolate," confirm the on call role actually has that permission in production — the playbook must match the rbac ../rbac and authorization reality. backup verification : restores in the playbook come from backups that have been test restored to a known good point, not merely "scheduled." playbook cx after an incident : after a drill or real incident, the retrospective produced a measurable edit step, tool, or owner — not just a meeting's minutes. related systems security operations center soc ../security operations center soc — where detection runs and where playbook execution is orchestrated endpoint security architecture playbook owners and affected cis get versioned47:Tbd9,integration patterns the five classic ways to connect systems — file transfer, shared database, rpc, messaging, and shared events — and how to pick between them. enterprise-architect solution-architect backend integration enterprise-architecture  what this system is every integration in your estate is one of a small family of patterns. gregor hohpe's "enterprise integration patterns" named the classical four; event streaming and webhooks modernized them. when an integration frustrates you "why is nothing happening?", "did the file arrive?" , the root cause is almost always a pattern property mismatch — you built a file transfer guarantor that needs messaging's delivery semantics. the five families pattern coupling delivery semantics latency classic use file transfer loose files at least once by poll+checksum batch minutes hours erp/legacy exchange, bank statements shared database tightest schema shared in db, locks copied real time ish internal monoliths, reporting rpc / streaming rpc tight interface shared at most once; caller handles the rest lowest service apis, grpc, sync calls messaging loose payload contract at least once + retries + dlq milliseconds work queues, fan out shared event stream very loose schema as weak contract at least once + replay milliseconds batch pub/sub, cdc, event sourcing how to choose the two axes that explain most failures synchronous vs asynchronous : rpc/webhooks are sync — the caller waits and must handle failure itself. messaging and cdc are async — nothing waits, but "eventually" means retries, ordering caveats, and idempotency. push vs pull : webhooks push source owns delivery, hard to retrofit , files and queues pull or poll. if a receiver is offline for hours, only pull/queue patterns survive without data loss. trade off cheat sheet i want… pattern to reach for guarantee i must accept immediate response, simple ops rpc/webhook duplicates possible, caller owns retries durable, exactly one ish work message queue + idempotency at least once, ordering per key many consumers see everything pub/sub ordering per partition, replay responsibility no app changes cdc schema events, log footprint auditable, human greppable file transfer batch latency, folder drift related systems message queue delivery ../message queue delivery — the messaging work horse pattern pub/sub and event streaming ../pub sub event streaming — the many consumer family member webhook integrations ../webhook integrations — the synchronous push pattern in — so the device is corporate owned, supervised , and policy controlled from day one. this is the apple answer to windows' domain join. how enrollment actually happens key actors: 1. abm — the apple's dep/mdm protocol. 3. supervised — the elevated state apple gives corporate claimed devices: allows config profiles like "always on vpn", deep restrictions, and app store only app installs that cannot be overridden. supervised vs personal devices personal device byod supervised/abm corporate belongs to the user the company config depth limited work profile ish controls full: restrictions, payloads, app lockdown app install user freedom only approved, mdm approved apps wipe user's consent full remote wipe at any time umbrella light touch heavy, always on controls why "app store only" is the interesting security lever on a supervised device, the mdm can forbid everything except the app store — killing sideloaded/signed app attack surface and rogue marketplaces in one boolean. combined supervised + compliant posture gates access authentication vs authorization ../authentication vs authorization — managed apple ids as identity49:T174a,itil change and release management from request to production safely: change types and the cab, change windows, release vs deployment, the service catalog, and the sla/ola/uc stack that makes service promises real. devops-engineer cloud-engineer enterprise-architect solution-architect itsm change workflow service-catalog  what this system is change enablement is the governance layer that lets an organisation modify a service without breaking it . it decides who may authorise what , when, and with how much ceremony, then release and deployment management carries the approved change into production. the question every change answers: what risk do we absorb, and who signs for it? a change is not the same as a release. the change is the decision and the plan what changes, with what risk, on whose authority ; the release is the assembly a build that bundles one or more approved changes ; the deployment is the act of putting that release into a live environment. governance lives on the change; mechanics live on the release. change types: three paths, three weights type authority ceremony use for standard pre approved in the catalogue none per change — it's already authorised password resets, adding a user to a group, routine patching runs normal change advisory board cab assess, approve, schedule, review service or infra changes with real blast radius emergency emergency cab ecab , or after the fact if time critical compressed or retroactive fixing a p1 outage, critical security patch, urgent data recovery standard changes are the design goal: if a change is routine, you shouldn't need a meeting every time — pre authorise once and let it flow. normal changes are where the risk conversation happens. emergency changes exist to be broken by design; the discipline is the mandatory post implementation review, where an emergency that becomes a habit is diagnosed. the change lifecycle 1. request — a ticket with description, rationale, owner, and target environment. vague requests get bounced before the cab ever sees them. 2. assess — impact and probability, what could break, and — critically — the backout plan : how you get back to the previous state. 3. authorise — the cab or the pre authorised standard path signs off with conditions. the keyword in the minutes is always "approved with the named mitigations". 4. dispatch — schedule into a change window with a start, an end, and an owner of the window ; late overruns are failures. 5. build + test — the release is assembled via ci/cd pipelines ../ci cd pipelines and verified against the plan. 6. deploy — the change leaves the cab's hands; the deployment team executes with freeze discipline. 7. review — post implementation review confirms what actually happened, what deviated, and whether the change page learned anything for the next one. the cab: who signs for risk a change advisory board is not a rubber stamp and not a bottleneck — it's the calibrated, delegated decision point. it works best when it's small and relevant: a service owner, the team whose environment is changing, an ops or infra lead, a security or architecture rep when the risk says so, and the change manager as facilitator. its output is a decision the record can point to: date, attendees, conditions, and the named accountable owner. emergencies get the ecab — or documented after the fact authorisation when the window cannot wait. the service catalog and service requests service catalog is the menu of what it actually sells — the user visible catalog email, vpn, crm mapped to the technical catalog that delivers it exchange, gateway, the crm app . every catalog item should state its price in business terms: availability, capacity, continuity, security . service requests a new laptop, an account unlock, a software install are the standard change pathway made visible to users — pre authorised, no cab, fulfilled against slas. the catalog and the request catalogue are the same lever: what you're willing to promise and what you're willing to do quickly without a meeting . slas, olas, and ucs — making promises real the service level stack is the contract ladder between "what we promise" and "who actually has to deliver it": layer between example sla service provider and customer 99.9% availability; p1 response in 15 minutes ola two internal teams networking resolves tickets within 4 hours of escalation uc provider and external supplier vendor patches the firewall within 3 business days tracing this ladder is how "the sa promised 99.9%" becomes a real, auditable, assigned obligation — and it's exactly where most sla brokenness hides: the ola and uc limbs of the chain were never written down. tester checklist take an approved normal change and confirm the backout plan is concrete: who to call, what command, how long to revert, and a test for "we are back". verify the standard change catalogue actually covers the routine things people do, and that a standard change never needs a cab meeting. drill an emergency change: trigger it during the outage, confirm the retro authorisation got recorded and a post implementation review is mandatory, not optional. audit change windows: does the deployment record show the window's real start and end, and who was accountable for the overrun? walk an sla to its uc: pick one public promise and confirm an ola + uc exist that would actually be breached first. related systems itil incident and problem management ../itil incident and problem management — where change fixes land after root cause, and where war room changes originate ci/cd pipelines ../ci cd pipelines — the build/test/release mechanics behind step 5 of the lifecycle devops delivery loop ../devops delivery loop — the cadence that wants standard changes automated, not cab'd monitoring and observability ../monitoring and observability — the alerting baseline that post implementation review borrows to verify a change is clean4a:T12df,itil incident and problem management restore fast, then stop it recurring: the incident lifecycle and priority matrix, problem management's chase after root cause, and how the known-error pipeline shrinks the next outage. devops-engineer cloud-engineer enterprise-architect itsm operations workflow reliability  what this system is itil splits operations into two parallel tracks. incident management restores service after an unplanned interruption — speed is the whole game. problem management attacks the unknown root cause behind one or many incidents — permanence is the whole game. every incident gets restored; only the repeat or unexplained ones earn a problem record and the root cause work that follows. the two tracks hand off deliberately: an incident can be closed as "workaround applied" while the problem underneath stays open. that separation is what lets you restore fast and stay honest that a real fix is still owed. the incident lifecycle 1. detect — a monitoring alarm, a user report, or a ticket from a linked team. detection latency is the first source of lost time. 2. log + categorise — every incident gets a record; categorisation decides which team owns it. 3. prioritise — impact how many users / services are hurt × urgency how fast it hurts → priority and sla clock. 4. diagnose — find the firing component and the likely cause; escalate when the diagnosis stalls or the sla clock runs hot. 5. resolve + restore — apply a fix or a workaround ; the goal is restoration, not permafix. verify the service actually recovered. 6. close + review — the incident is closed only when the user confirms and monitoring confirms. a review decides whether a problem record is owed. the priority matrix priority impact urgency example sla target p1 critical whole service down immediate payments processing fails minutes to respond, <4h restore p2 high major feature down fast checkout broken for all users 15min respond, same day p3 medium workaround exists normal one region slow, others fine hours, ticket tracked p4 low cosmetic low wrong label in ui best effort, queue priority drives where the sla clock runs and who gets woken. the classic failure is a p3 that nobody re grades into a p1 when usage spikes — re prioritisation is a first class step , not an afterthought. escalation: functional and hierarchical functional escalation — the record moves to a different team that is better equipped network, database, vendor because the diagnosis crossed a boundary. hierarchical escalation — up the management chain because the sla is at risk, resources are contested, or the user facing impact demands an exec to unblock. the rule: escalate to enable , not to comfort. each step should add a decision maker or a skilled responder, and the incident record has to travel with the handoff. the incident to problem pipeline problem management: the cure loop 1. problem record — opened from a major incident, a flood of similar incidents, or a proactive trend scan. 2. root cause analysis — the technique 5 whys, fishbone, log forensics matters less than actually reaching a cause you can point at. 3. known error + workaround — the name itil gives a diagnosed cause that still has no fix: document it and drop it in the knowledge base so the next incident resolves in minutes, not hours. 4. fix via change — the actual repair goes through change and release management ../itil change and release management so it lands with the same governance as any other production change. the output that compounds is the knowledge base: every ticket is a candidate for a runbook entry, and every known error buys the incident desk time later. tester checklist run a p1 drill: alarm → incident → first response inside the sla target, with the record surviving the handoff. forced a repeat alarm scenario same alert twice in a day and confirm the second incident auto links to the existing problem record. verify a workaround closed incident correctly leaves its problem record open and re flagged at review. re grade test: change an incident from p3 to p1 and confirm the sla clock and escalation path actually re arm. audit escalation: pick a closed major incident and confirm every handoff preserved the timeline and added a named owner. related systems itil change and release management ../itil change and release management — where problem fixes and incident war rooms get approved change records monitoring and observability ../monitoring and observability — the alerting layer that provides most incident detection security operations center soc ../security operations center soc — the sibling pipeline for attacks , sharing telemetry with the outage side devops delivery loop ../devops delivery loop — closing the loop from incident feedback back into the delivery pipeline4b:Tcd7,jira and agile delivery epics, stories, tasks, bugs — the issue types and workflow states that turn roadmap intent into shipped, verified work. developer tester devops-engineer enterprise-architect devops workflow agile  what this system is jira and its atlassian siblings is the issue tracker that records what must change and why , from epics down to tasks, and tracks each through a state machine: who owns it, whether it's in progress, whether it's verified. its superpower is traceability : every commit, pr, and test points back at a ticket, so "shipped but forgotten" becomes rare. the issue types know what each is for type level when you create one epic container a big initiative spanning releases a checkout rewrite story vertical slice a user visible outcome with acceptance criteria task work item engineer work that isn't a user story ci setup bug defect something that should work but doesn't sub task sibling breaking a story into trackable pieces the trap is inventing issue types every sprint — stick to epic/story/task/bug and let fields priority, labels, sprints carry the nuance. the workflow states a state machine you control in review means "reviewer owns it now" — move it when you send it, not when it's approved. done vs closed : done code merged is not verified — a truthful team distinguishes shipped from confirmed in prod . blocked is a first class state with a visible cause, or it silently rots. scrum vs kanban boards scrum kanban sprints fixed timeboxes 1 2 weeks continuous flow, no timebox planning sprint goal + committed scope pull based, wip limits metrics velocity, burndown cycle time, throughput, wip fits predictable product iterations ops/support streams tickets, incidents both are delivery lenses over the same issue types — the choice is about flow, not about which tool. definition of done is the quality contract a story is done when its acceptance criteria pass , code is merged with review, ci builds and tests green, and it's deployed and verified . anything less is "in progress wearing a disguise." the verified state exists precisely to catch stories marked done that never got a second look in prod. tester checklist take a story whose acceptance criteria exist and confirm every criterion maps to a test that actually ran not a "should be ok" . follow a bug end to end: reproduce → in progress → in review → verified on the fix branch vs prod. audit stale states: how many issues sit in done code merged but never reach verified ? that's your leak confirm the code↔ticket link both ways: the commit references the ticket, and the ticket links the pr/commit. related systems git and version control ../git and version control — the commits that reference these tickets ci/cd pipelines ../ci cd transfer, and exit is an event that adds or strips access — automatically. enterprise-architect security-architect devops-engineer identity security workflow  what this system is jml joiner / mover / leaver is the operating model for identity: three kinds of life change events, each with a defined set of access consequences. joining grants access, moving rescopes it, leaving revokes it. done manually, these are the source of ghost accounts and admin sprawl; done as an event driven pipeline, they become invisible and reliable. the three phases phase trigger the access move failure if skipped joiner first day in hr accounts created in the right groups, right roles manual "set up" tickets, late access, shadow ids mover role/team change old groups removed, new groups added, privilege rescoped accumulated stale rights "they used to…" leaver termination date sessions killed, accounts suspended/deleted, privilege stripped, license reclaimed orphan accounts = the 1 audit finding each is a state change in hr that triggers a chain: hr → identity store → scim ../scim identity provisioning provisioning → sso ../single sign on sso session revocation → pim ../privileged identity management privilege deactivation. the leaver chain, end to end notice the date on the termination event : identity should act at the boundary, not at "whenever someone remembers." why jml is really an access governance feature access reviews : with jml wired up, reviews become "does the current state match the events we processed?" instead of "document everything in a spreadsheet." orphan detection : accounts that never got a termination event are the canary — a monitoring query for "active past 90 days + no supervisor" is your safety net. separation of duties : a lemma of jml is that moves don't add — a promotion shouldn't keep the leaver tier rights of the old job unless that is a deliberate decision rbac ../rbac and authorization . tester checklist join drill: a new hire event with a future start date must create accounts, roles, and app memberships exactly at start not a day early . move drill: change a user's team and confirm old group access removes , new adds — and the intersection is re reviewed. leaver drill with a timer: at 17:00 termination, by 17:00:30 all sso sessions are dead and scim accounts suspended. anti pattern check: is there any manual "give this user access right now" path that by passes the event chain? kill it. related systems identity and access management ../identity and access management — the iam layer jml orchestrates scim identity that trigger the jml chain4d:Tbb8,jwt access tokens the three base64 parts of a jwt, how signature verification works, and the stateless-vs-stateful trade-off behind token-based apis. developer frontend backend security-architect security api frontend  what this system is a jwt json web token is a signed, self contained statement like "this is user 42, role admin, valid until noon" . because it is signed, an api can trust the token's claims without asking anyone — that statelessness is both the benefit and the danger. the three parts part contents purpose header alg , typ says which algorithm signed it payload claims: sub , exp , iss , aud , role , scope the identity + permissions signature digest over header.payload with the secret integrity + authenticity the whole token is base64url header + "." + base64url payload + "." + base64url signature — remember: payload is readable by anyone it's only encoded, not encrypted . never put secrets in a jwt. how verification should work the api proves the token was minted by the idp by checking the signature with a shared secret hmac or the idp's public key rsa/ec . once it trusts the signature, it trusts the claims — unless it needs to check revocation. the stateless vs stateful trade off stateless jwt session stateful where identity lives inside the token in the server's session store server lookup on each request none fast, scalable yes redis / db hit logout / revoke hard — must wait for exp or keep a blocklist instant — delete the session info exposure payload readable if token leaks nothing in the token if you need instant revocation logout, account ban , you'll add a token blocklist/denylist or keep short lived tokens with refresh tokens see oauth/oidc guide . the attacks that keep failing teams alg: none — never accept unsigned tokens; pin the algorithm server side. algorithm confusion — rs256 signed tokens replayed as hs256 will "verify" only if the api wrongly uses the public key as the hmac secret. weak secret brute force — a guessable hmac secret is a signed anything pass. stale exp — check expiry, always, on every endpoint. aud / iss not checked — tokens minted for another app or issued by a different idp are accepted. token in a url query string — leaks into logs and browser history; keep tokens in memory / short lived, never in localstorage unless fully understood. tester checklist try the alg=none variant and a tampered payload with the same signature — both must be rejected. verify that exp , aud , and iss checks actually fire craft tokens with bad values . confirm tokens are never logged, not even in api gateway request logs. check the refresh token rotation: a used refresh token must not be reusable. related systems oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — where jwts are minted and delivered authentication vs authorization ../authentication vs authorization — verifying who is only the first question session cookies and csrf ../session cookies and csrf — servers, databases, ci runners, network appliances, cloud instances. "managing linux" at scale isn't sshing into hosts; it's making hosts interchangeable so a crashed one disappears and a fresh one looks identical. the two mental models old way pets new way cattle / immutable ssh in, fix it live never patch a live server; build a new image drift over months config is code, auditable in git, reproduced everywhere hand written playbooks idempotent, versioned, tested in ci servers have names & moods servers have roles and are disposable most enterprises run a mix: stateful databases still demand care, stateless tiers are freely rotated. the rule that holds everywhere: the source of truth is code, not a shell session. the pieces that matter the patch cycle the drumbeat that never stops patch rings mirror windows autopatch: a canary host gets the update first; only on success does it roll to broad. a bad kernel in one ring doesn't take down production. secrets never ship in images or repos — pulled at boot from a vault/secret manager identity best practice ../authentication vs authorization with short lived credentials. reboots are planned, drained, and verified — orchestrated, never chaotic. vulnerability triage is a risk calculation, not an all or nothing: you patch what's reachable first. tester checklist rebuild a host from the golden image and diff it against peers — drift should be zero or a documented exception. kill a stateless node mid traffic and confirm orchestration reconstitutes it without user visible impact. roll a deliberately bad package to the canary ring and verify the ring's monitors catch it before broad rollout. verify a fresh host cannot boot with secrets available in its filesystem secrets come from the vault only . related systems windows in the enterprise ../windows in the enterprise — same fleet every service gets a service identity api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — what the servers are actually serving dockerizing applications ../dockerizing applications — the container runtime that turns a host into a service platform4f:T11d4,llm agents and workflows agent loops, tool calling, memory, and guardrails: the orchestration patterns that turn a single llm call into a multi-step workflow. developer backend tester solution-architect ai api architecture workflow  what this system is a single llm call is a function: prompt in, text out. an ai workflow is a system that decides which call to make next. the deciding force is usually the model itself — it outputs a structured tool call , your code executes the tool, feeds the result back, and repeats until the model emits a final answer. that loop is the agent pattern. the alternative is a workflow dag where you, not the model, decide the order of steps. almost every production system is a mix: hard coded structure where steps are predictable, agent loop where the path is not. agent loop vs workflow dag workflow dag agent open loop who picks the next step you, at design time the model, at runtime predictability high — fixed pipeline low — model can go anywhere debuggability deterministic, replayable needs session logging + eval cost bounded, visible unbounded unless capped fits classify → extract → answer explore, plan, and use many tools failure mode rigid — breaks on unseen inputs drift — hallucinated or repeated tool calls the rule of thumb: fix the loop only as much as the problem needs . a workflow for jobs with a known shape; an agent when the shape is unknown until the model sees the input. teams that default to "full agent" for everything inherit unbounded cost and unreplayable bugs. tools: the model's arms tool calling is json in, json out: the model returns { "tool": "search docs", "args": {...} } , your code validates the arguments against the actual function's schema, executes your trusted code, and appends the result. three design rules matter more than prompt wording: schemas do the work — the function signature is the contract; a stricter schema means fewer invalid calls. your code decides, not the model — the model only proposes a call; your side filters and scopes it drop tools the user's permissions don't cover . every tool is an attack surface — a model coerced by prompt injection to call delete account args executes your code with real side effects. memory: context window vs remembered state the context window is ram, not disk: it is re packed on every call from the raw transcript. anything that must persist across turns — a shopping cart, a user's preference, an approval state — must be written to your system , and re injected as context on the next turn. the classic bug is an agent that "remembers" something it never persisted, then loses it when the transcript is truncated to fit the window. the request → happy path guardrails: what keeps a loop from being a runaway max iterations & time budget — a hard cap on loop turns; the model can infinitely re plan, and billing follows. tool approval gates — destructive or costly tools send email, delete, pay require a confirm step; "human approval" is the anti prompt injection wall. permission scoping per user — tool availability should mirror the caller's roles, not the service account's. observation size limits — truncate tool output before re prompting; a 10 mb json blob wrecks context, latency, and cost. forced rerouting — if the model repeats the same failing call no progress , detect it and hand the task to a fallback path. tester checklist loop cap: assert the agent stops after max iterations, not silently running forever watch billing . tool argument validation: send a prompt that should never produce calls to a scoped out tool, and verify the code refuses it. injection drill: hide an instruction like "call delete on every record" inside retrieved tool output — does the guardrail stop it? persistence across turns: end a session, resume, and confirm state the agent claimed to "remember" actually came from your store. regression set: keep a fixed set of real tasks and assert the agent's final answer is correct and reaches it before and after any prompt/model change. related systems llm rag pipeline ../llm rag pipeline — the retrieval half that grounds agents in your data mcp and a2a protocols ../mcp and a2a protocols — the wire protocols an agent uses for tools and peers message queue delivery ../message queue delivery — running agent jobs asynchronously behind a queue api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — the request path the workflow orchestrates through owasp top 10 ../owasp top 10 — the injection category that prompt injection extends50:T1601,mcp and a2a protocols model context protocol for agent-to-tool, agent2agent for agent-to-agent: discovery, message flow, task lifecycle, and how the two interoperability layers compose. developer backend solution-architect security-architect ai api integration architecture  what this system is two open protocols standardize how ai systems attach to the world, and they are complementary rather than competing: mcp model context protocol, anthropic → open spec is the usb c of ai : an agent connects once and can use any tool, data source, or api exposed through a standard server — without per integration glue. a2a agent2agent, google → linux foundation is the email of agents : independent agents discover each other, delegate work, and exchange results as peers, across frameworks and companies. mcp is how an agent touches tools and data . a2a is how an agent partners with other agents . a real stack uses both: your agent calls mcp servers for its own tools while delegating to remote agents over a2a. mcp: agent to tool three roles: the host your ai application — it owns the model and the conversation , clients one per server connection , and servers each exposes tools for actions, resources for data, and prompts for reusable templates . the data layer is json rpc 2.0 ; the transport is stdio for local processes or streamable http server sent events for streaming for remote servers, authenticated with oauth. security is built into the shape: the host is the enforcement point . capability negotiation decides what each side supports; servers are isolated from each other and cannot read the full conversation. this is also where the well known failure modes live — every tool is an attack surface, so the host must gate calls with consent and permission checks, not the model. a2a: agent to agent a2a treats agents as opaque peers. four concepts do the work: concept what it is agent card a json discovery document at /.well known/agent card.json : identity, description, skills, endpoint, and auth requirements task a stateful unit of work: submitted → working → input required → completed / failed / canceled message one turn of a conversation, carrying parts text, file, or structured data artifact a deliverable produced by a task, also composed of parts, streamable incrementally input required is the mechanism for a remote agent to ask for clarification mid task, which is what makes long running delegation interactive. tasks can be polled, subscribed to over sse, or pushed to a webhook when they outlive a connection. the primary binding is json rpc 2.0 over https , with grpc and rest as alternates — deliberately boring plumbing. the two layers, compared mcp a2a relationship client server: an agent uses a server peer: agents delegate to agents unit of work tool call / resource read task with a lifecycle discovery capability negotiation during initialize agent card at a well known url transport stdio, streamable http json rpc over https, grpc, sse, webhooks authentication oauth for remote http oauth 2.0 data exchanged tool arguments and structured results messages and artifacts with typed parts trust servers stay isolated from the conversation remote agents are untrusted peers if mcp is "how an agent uses a capability", a2a is "how agents partner on a task". an mcp server is reachable inside an agent's world; an a2a remote lives outside it, behind an agent card. how they compose the rule of thumb: expose capabilities as mcp servers, front agent pairs with a2a cards. a support agent might use mcp against the ticket system internally, then delegate an order status lookup over a2a to a billing agent on the other side of a company boundary. cross boundary security notes mcp : the host gates every tool call — a compromised or prompt injected server must not reach tools the user didn't consent to. verify tool schemas before they influence execution. a2a : remote agents are untrusted peers . validate the agent card, authenticate with oauth, and route delegated work — especially anything that cascades back into your mcp tools — through the user's permission scope, not a broad service account. supply chain : capability metadata tool definitions, agent cards is attacker influenceable; treat changes as config and review them. tester checklist mcp flow : stand up a mock server and verify initialize → tools/list → tools/call , including streaming over streamable http. isolation : confirm one mcp server cannot read another server's context or the full conversation. a2a lifecycle : exercise submitted → working → completed plus failed and canceled , and confirm terminal states end any stream. resilience : kill a task mid run and reconnect — get /subscription must recover state without losing artifacts. input required : a remote agent asking for clarification mid task must surface to the user and resume the same task. auth : reject calls to remote mcp/http endpoints and a2a remotes without valid credentials. cross layer scope : a task delegated over a2a must not let the remote agent invoke mcp tools beyond the requesting user's permissions. related systems llm agents and workflows ../llm agents and workflows — the agent loops these protocols wire up llm rag pipeline ../llm rag pipeline — a retrieval engine commonly exposed as an mcp resource oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the auth layer both protocols lean on workload identity and mtls ../workload identity and mtls — machine to machine trust for remote servers api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — the json rpc/http machinery underneath51:T86d,message queue delivery why at-least-once delivery exists, how an outbox pattern makes publishes atomic-ish, and where duplicates actually come from. developer backend tester architecture api  what this system is when an api must not process work in the request response cycle sending emails, resizing images, running ai jobs , it writes a message to a queue and returns immediately. a worker later picks the message up. the price of decoupling is a new failure mode: messages are delivered at least once, so your handler must be idempotent . why “at least once” most brokers sqs, rabbitmq, kafka guarantee at least once, not exactly once: 1. worker reads message and starts processing a side effect email sent . 2. worker crashes before sending the ack . 3. broker sees no ack, assumes failure, and redelivers — or the consumer group rebalances. 4. same message is processed again → the email is sent twice. how to stay safe technique what it does idempotency key a stable header like order 42 ; the handler stores processed keys and skips repeats outbox pattern api writes the business row and the message in the same db transaction; a relay publishes rows from the outbox table — no dual write race dead letter queue dlq messages that fail after n retries move aside for inspection instead of looping forever tester checklist replay a message manually — the second run must be a no op. kill a worker mid handling and confirm redelivery + no partial side effects. verify the dlq buries poison messages instead of blocking the queue. check ordering guarantees: are you relying on exactly once ordering you were never promised? related systems api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — the request that writes the message llm rag pipeline ../llm rag pipeline — ai jobs routed through queues for async inference pub/sub and event streaming ../pub sub event streaming — the fan out sibling of competing consumer queues integration patterns ../integration patterns — where messaging fits among the five integration families webhook integrations ../webhook integrations — receivers that deliver to an http callback instead of a queue52:Tbd5,micro-frontends composing an independent checkout app and catalog app into one page at runtime — module federation, shells, and when the split is worth its cost. frontend solution-architect enterprise-architect web frontend architecture  what this system is one large frontend, one team, one big release. micro frontends split the ui into independently owned apps checkout, catalog, accounts composed into a single page at runtime — each with its own repo, deploy, and release cadence. the benefit is organizational autonomy , not technical: the compounding cost is versioning, shared state, and cross app consistency. build time vs runtime composition build time composition runtime module federation when each app is npm built into the shell remote apps are fetched at runtime shared runtime version skew one version, one release each remote flows on its own release failure blast a bad dep blocks the whole build a broken remote can degrade a region of the page rollback one atomic deploy per remote rollback, coordination required best for team boundaries with shared release large orgs with genuinely independent teams module federation webpack/rspack, vite plugins is the current runtime tooling: the shell remote imports modules and shares libraries react, router so duplicates don't balloon the bundle. how a page is composed the three agreements that keep it from collapsing 1. shared runtime contract : which react/router versions, how remotes are exposed, and how events/navigation are typed — agreed once, enforced by the shell. 2. state ownership : no global store written by many remotes. each module owns its state; cross module data goes through the shell's event bus or an api, never shared mutable objects. 3. design tokens, not ad hoc css : styles flow from a shared token/cdn source so visual drift against each other stays low without a monorepo. when it's right and when it isn't right : 3 teams on one product, genuine release independence needed, a real shared shell is maintained. wrong : a two person team "for flexibility" — you pay federating cost with no autonomy win. if the smoke test becomes "did all remotes load today?", reconsider; you turned a feature app into a micro spof. tester checklist load every route with one remote deliberately down — the page must fail graceful that module degrades , not produce a blank shell. verify version mix works: a new checkout release alongside an old catalog release on the same shell. measure bundle share: confirm shared modules react/core are fetched once, not duplicated per remote. cross module navigation must hold state and deep links across remote boundaries. related systems frontend frameworks and rendering ../frontend ../cloud networking and hybrid connectivity — routing/versioning problems that grow with every added frontier53:T2104,microsoft entra id architecture the tenant, directory, identities, and trust boundaries of entra id (azure ad) — plus external identities, b2b, b2c, and the tenant-separation decisions that keep real estates safe. security-architect enterprise-architect cloud-engineer backend identity azure security enterprise-architecture saas  what this system is microsoft entra id formerly azure active directory is microsoft's cloud identity provider : the directory of users, devices, and apps behind microsoft 365, azure, and thousands of saas apps. it is the identity backbone of most modern windows/office estates, and the mental model that unlocks it is tenant : every organization gets an isolated tenant a directory of identities + the policies that govern them , and everything else — apps, saas, devices, external collaborators — hangs off that one boundary. "entra id architecture" is mostly the art of deciding what a tenant is and how identities flow across trust boundaries. the core objects that make the directory object what it is the failure if ignored tenant one isolated directory a .onmicrosoft.com domain , the security boundary two orgs merged into one tenant = one boundary confusion user directory principal with identity + attributes + entitlements guest/vendor users dumped into the same object model as employees group member container used for assignment assigning apps directly to users instead of groups = unmanageable sprawl service principal / enterprise app the instance of an app in your tenant the "who" that gets permissions confusing the app registration the definition with the principal the grants device identity enrolled devices with their own identity for zero trust ../zero trust architecture devices trusted by account only — posture checks have nothing to attach to app registration the app's metadata: client id, redirects, api scopes client secrets committed to repos; wrong redirect uris the three that trip everyone: tenant the boundary , app registration vs service principal definition vs instance , and group based assignment the only thing that scales . get those three right and most entra architecture is sound. the identity lifecycle: join, access, leave users are born from an hr/source of truth, assigned to groups, provisioned into saas via scim ../scim identity provisioning , and on leaving are disabled, session revoked, and deprovisioned. the loop is the same as joiner mover leaver ../joiner mover leaver everywhere; entra's twist is that conditional access evaluates posture at sign in , so the directory is also an enforcement point, not just a list. external identities: b2b, b2c, and cross tenant b2b collaboration is for business collaborators : you invite an external user who keeps their home identity into your tenant as a guest . entra stores a shadow guest object, but sign in happens at the guest's own idp — you never manage their password. guests then get app access like anyone, scoped through conditional access. this is the model for vendors, partners, and contractors. b2c azure ad b2c is the opposite: a separate consumer tenant where you create and own end user identities for your public/customer facing apps social sign in, self service registration . it is not the same tenant as your employees — that split is deliberate and mandatory for governance. cross tenant access is when two orgs federate or trust each other's tenants m365 shared channels, guest sync via scim ../scim identity provisioning ; entra now manages this with cross tenant access settings and an external collaboration policy that whitelists/blacklists which tenants can be invited. the b2b vs b2c decision in one line: b2b = "your vendor can see our sharepoint," b2c = "our customers have accounts in our app." mixing consumer and employee identity in one directory is the classic governance disaster — which is exactly why b2c is its own tenant type. tenant topology: the decision that defines the architecture decision choose when the price single tenant one org, one governance model, default for most everything shares one boundary; guest bloat if partners pile in multiple tenants regulatory isolation, subsidiaries, b2c vs employee cross tenant sharing guests, channels, sync is real ongoing cost b2c as its own tenant any consumer facing app must consciously keep employee and consumer identity apart the architecture mistake to avoid: letting tenant topology grow by accident. every "just invite them into our tenant" adds a guest; every "can we sync their staff?" adds a cross tenant rule; before long the boundary is ambiguous. decide the topology deliberately, apply an external collaboration policy, and treat tenant boundaries as the defense in depth ../defense in depth perimeter they are. conditional access: the enforcement engine conditional access is where entra becomes zero trust enforcement: every sign in is evaluated against policies that combine who user/group , what app , where location , and device posture . the signature policies: require mfa for admins , require a compliant device , block legacy authentication , location based risk , and sign in frequency/session controls . it is the mechanism that makes "trusted device + mfa + right location" a requirement instead of a wish, and it's why endpoint security ../endpoint security architecture and entra are one system, not two. the failure modes an architect owns tenant sprawl : a tenant per pilot, per team, per "we might need it" — cross tenant sharing becomes ungovernable. mixing b2b, b2c, and employees : guests and customers and staff in one directory with one policy surface — the governance disaster b2c's separate tenant exists to prevent. assigning apps directly to users : no groups, so every app is a user by user list; onboarding/offboarding means touching hundreds of rows. no conditional access : sign in works from any device, any location, no mfa for admins — the estate runs on trust and passwords. legacy auth left on : protocols that bypass mfa basic auth, imap/smtp still enabled, silently defeating every policy. app registration/principal confusion : a developer's app registered with broad delegated permissions that outlive its purpose; scopes never trimmed. guest bloat : external collaborators invited into the employee tenant with no lifecycle policy, no expiry, no review. tester checklist guest lifecycle : invite an external user, confirm their sign in happens at their own idp, that they can reach only what's assigned, and that removing them from the group immediately blocks access. b2b vs b2c boundary : confirm consumer identities live in a separate b2c tenant and can never resolve into the employee directory. conditional access realness : craft a sign in from a disallowed location/device and confirm the policy blocks or requires mfa , not merely logs. mfa for admins : a global admin sign in without mfa must be impossible in test — the policy applies even from a "trusted" device. scim provisioning : change a user's group membership and confirm the downstream saas app's entitlements sync within the sla; on offboarding, deprovisioning lands. tenant topology audit : list all tenants, guests, and cross tenant rules and confirm each is deliberate and covered by policy — no accidental boundaries. app principal hygiene : enumerate service principal permissions and confirm none exceed their current purpose least privilege, scopes trimmed . related systems identity and access management iam ../identity and access management — the discipline entra id implements for microsoft estates single sign on sso ../single to everything in the tenant54:T114a,microsoft graph api one api for all of microsoft 365: users, mail, drives, teams, intune — the unified graph model, delegated vs app-only auth, and the paging and throttling rules you live by. developer backend enterprise-architect security-architect api cloud identity architecture  what this system is microsoft graph is the single api for microsoft 365 : instead of a separate api for exchange, onedrive, teams, sharepoint, entra id, and intune, everything hangs off one service rooted at https://graph.microsoft.com . company data — users, mail, files, chats, devices, groups — is a graph of entities with relationships you traverse with rest verbs or graphql style select lists . the model: entities and relationships the graph is a true graph : navigate relationships in either direction get /me , get /me/directreports , get /groups/{id}/members . key entry points: resource how you reach it me / users get /me , get /users/{id} , get /users?$filter=... groups get /groups , get /groups/{id}/members mail get /me/messages?$top=10 , post /me/sendmail calendar get /me/events , booking resources drives/files get /me/drive/root/children , sharepoint sites & lists teams/chats get /me/chats , post /chats/{id}/messages devices intune get /devicemanagement/manageddevices the $select , $filter , $expand , $top , $orderby , and $skip odata query options are the whole game: they control what comes back, so you fetch exactly what you need instead of dumping a whole entity. delegated vs application app only auth delegated : the app acts as a signed in user, with scopes like mail.read that the user or tenant admin consents to. the token represents user + app. application only : no user — the app has tenant level roles e.g. admin granted mail.read for the whole org . this is the dangerous one: an app only token reaches everything its app level permissions allow, no individual user in the loop. the recurring incident: an app registered with application permissions when it only needed delegated — one bad consent and a background job can read every mailbox. least privilege means choosing the narrowest model that does the job. the api mechanics: tokens, throttling, pagination auth : always a bearer token from msal/oauth 2.0 — never the user's password. tokens are short lived; refresh before expiry. pagination : graph returns @odata.nextlink — follow it for the next page. don't re run the same query; don't assume a single response is the whole set. throttling : graph has rate limits hit 429 with a retry after . the contract: fetch less, cache more, and honor backoff — a hammering batch app gets slowed by design, and retrying too fast makes it worse. batch : $batch lets you combine up to 20 requests into one — the answered prayer for chatty apps that would otherwise pad a round trip per call. the mistakes repeatedly remembered using app only permissions for a workflow that acts on a single user's behalf — every mailbox becomes readable. ignoring pagination and paginating by hand — a 10k row migration that assumed one response is a classic. no throttling handling: re firing instantly on 429 triggers the very backoff the api is enforcing. storing a user's password to "log in and sync mail" instead of using delegated auth properly. treating get /me as "any user": with app only auth, /me doesn't exist as a personal context — you must target /users/{id} . tester checklist confirm delegated vs app only: a background job must not have scopes it can't use, and an app only token must not be able to read get /me . hit a paginated endpoint and verify you follow @odata.nextlink end to end all pages collected, none skipped . hammer a throttled endpoint and verify your client honors retry after instead of spinning. test least privilege: a mail.read only app cannot write or read other data types; add the minimal scope and confirm a forbidden call returns the expected error. batch test: combine 20 small calls into one $batch and confirm it saves round trips without breaking errors. related systems oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the token flow graph's bearer header rides on salesforce, sap, jdbc, http, messaging "tiles, not coding the wire protocol" api manager policy + key + version management for exposed apis "the governance face your consumers touch" cloudhub mulesoft's managed hosting for mule apps "run it without running your own runtime fleet" anypoint exchange the shared hub of connectors, templates, and apis "the org wide library" the mental model: a flow is a chain of message processors — a listener receives an event, transformers map the payload, connectors call endpoints, and the result flows onward. every flow is inside a mule app, and the app is what gets deployed to cloudhub or to your own runtime. anatomy of a mule flow listeners trigger the flow: http/https, jms, file polling, timers, or message queues. dataweave is mulesoft's expression + transformation language — the thing you spend most design time in, mapping an xml invoice into a salesforce json opportunity. error handling is scoped per flow: on error propagate, retries, and a dead letter pattern keep a bad message from killing the pipeline. every flow tracks : mulesoft has baked in logging, metrics, and runtime health per app — "why did it fail" gets a traceable answer without you building observability. api led connectivity: the mulesoft doctrine mulesoft's flagship architecture is api led connectivity : integration apis are organized in three layers — system wraps a backend with a stable contract , process orchestrates business workflows across systems , experience shapes data for a specific consumer . the value proposition: a consumer changes once instead of touching every wiring. the pragmatic caution: three layers for tiny estates is enterprise theater — run the layers you actually have consumers for. mulesoft vs the alternatives oracle integration cloud ../oracle integration cloud for oracle centric fit better. mulesoft's user mains in a salesforce heavy world because of the deep salesforce connector and the exchange templates. the failure modes that plague ipaas projects spaghetti reborn : a mulesoft integration per point connection instead of per business capability — the hub grows but so does the tangle. re inventing the backend : flows that embed business rules that belong in the service behind the api. untested connectors : assuming the "salesforce upsert" connector handles fields you actually send — connectors still need real payload tests. runtime sprawl : every developer shipping their own cloudhub environment until cost and governance explode. ignoring idempotency and ordering : at least once over jms or retries means duplicate delivery silently creates duplicate orders. tester checklist flow trigger drill : call the listener endpoint s — http, queue, file drop — and confirm each path tracks and completes. transformation fidelity : run an xml→json dataweave map with dates, decimals, nulls, and arrays; confirm no drift and no silent loss. retry/dlq behavior : force the downstream connector to fail, let it retry with backoff, then confirm the failing message dead letters and is visible with its trace id. policy gate : expose an api through api manager and confirm an unauthenticated / over rate call is rejected by the policy before the flow runs. idempotency : deliver the same message twice and confirm no duplicate order is created downstream. runtime health : routing and bgp how a packet finds its way across the internet: routing tables, longest-prefix-match, ospf inside your network, and bgp between autonomous systems. backend enterprise-architect solution-architect network architecture  what this system is routing is how a byte for 8.8.8.8 leaves your server, crosses several companies' networks, and arrives at google's. every router keeps a routing table ; on arrival it looks up the destination, matches the longest prefix , and forwards on. the difficult part is populating those tables at internet scale — that is bgp's job. longest prefix match routers don't know the whole path — only the next hop. a table entry 10.0.0.0/16 → via 10.0.0.1 and 10.0.2.0/24 → via 10.0.0.9 both match 10.0.2.55 ; the /24 longest wins . this is how a default route 0.0.0.0/0 coexists with precise prefixes. routing type scope who builds the table convergence speed static routes your edge, small dcs you, by hand instant but manual to fix ospf igp inside one as / enterprise routers exchange link state fast seconds , scales to hundreds eigrp/rip cisco shops / tiny nets router metrics medium bgp egp between autonomous systems peers advertise prefixes minutes — engineered, not eager bgp: the internet's glue the internet is thousands of autonomous systems ases — companies with an as number and their own routing domain. bgp lets each as tell its neighbors "i can reach 1.2.3.0/24 via me ". every router independently picks the best path by as path length, then policy, then tie breakers. why routing breaks skills worth keeping blackholes : a route advertised but silently dropping traffic — the classic failure when a path dies but bgp doesn't know. slow convergence : bgp is policy first, speed later ; a session flap at a tier 1 isp can take minutes, and every dependency waits. prefix hijacking : mis advertised prefixes route traffic to the wrong as bgp has no built in auth; rpki / manrs ../zero trust architecture tries to fix that . as path prepending trick: adding your own as twice just to downgrade a path in someone's selection — traffic engineering by lying politely. tester checklist walk a real path with traceroute / mtr when users report latency — identify which hops add the ms. verify failover: disable the primary uplink and confirm traffic shifts to the backup within your expected slas seconds? minutes? . use a looking glass e.g. routeviews, your upstream's lg to confirm your prefixes are announced and not being suppressed. confirm your static routes fall back correctly when an interface flaps — a static route to a dead next hop forwards into the void unless you track reachability. related systems tcp/ip and tls ../tcp ip and tls — the packet and segment this routing carries zero trust architecture ../zero trust architecture — routing is reachability; authorization still needs zt layers sd wan and edge connectivity ../sd wan and edge connectivity — how enterprise branches steer over these paths dns and load balancing ../dns and load balancing — the address resolution that precedes the first hop5b:T756,oauth 2.0 and openid connect the authorization code flow with pkce, explained as a conversation between a browser, the app, and an identity provider. developer frontend backend tester api security architecture  what this system is logging in with “sign in with google” is easier to describe as a chat between three actors than as a sequence of claims. oauth 2.0 hands out tokens for accessing resources; openid connect oidc adds the id token that proves who you are. the three actors actor talks to example resource owner the user who approves access you client your application, wants access the spa / backend authorization server issues tokens, owns identities auth0, keycloak, okta key design decisions authorization code flow is the default for web apps: the code never shows to the browser, only the backend exchanges it for tokens, so refresh tokens stay server side. pkce proof key for code exchange works even on public clients: the browser sends a one time challenge of a secret it holds. the id token is signed json a jwt . verify the signature and audience before trusting it — anyone can mint an unsigned jwt. scopes e.g. openid , email , profile describe permission boundaries; grant the minimum set. what a tester watches for 1. token leakage — does the access token ever appear in a url query string or in browser storage for a public client? 2. state / pkce validation — can an attacker replay or swap authorization codes? 3. refresh token rotation — is a reused refresh token rotated or revoked? 4. expired tokens — does the client silently refresh or hard fail? does the resource server actually check expiry? related systems api request lifecycle ../api request lifecycle — how the bearer token flows through the chain on every request api gateway caching ../api gateway caching — caching authenticated responses without leaking private data5c:T1013,oauth grant types authorization code with pkce, client credentials, device flow, and refresh — the five ways to get an oauth token, who each is for, and how to pick (and test) the right one. developer backend frontend tester security api identity  what this system is oauth 2.0 issues tokens , but not all tokens are obtained the same way. a grant type is the recipe for how a client proves it may ask for a token — and picking the right one or the wrong one is the single most consequential decision in your auth design. there are five that matter; the rest are legacy footnotes. the five grants that matter grant who it's for how the token is obtained secret? authorization code + pkce web apps, spas, mobile — anything with a user browser redirects to idp, user consents, code exchanged for tokens pkce instead of a secret client credentials server to server, no user client signs with its own credentials, gets a token for itself yes — the client secret device flow tvs, clis, iot — no browser user opens a url on any device, enters a code no refresh token long lived sessions exchanged server side for new access tokens where the access token came from resource owner password legacy only, discouraged user's username+password sent to the idp never do this in new code the authorization code flow with pkce why pkce proof key for code exchange even when there's a client secret: the browser redirect carries the code , and a public client can't protect a secret the browser holds. the code verifier proves that whoever is exchanging the code is the same party that started the flow — it kills a whole class of code interception attacks. in modern oauth 2.1 , pkce is mandatory for everything . client credentials: the machine to machine grant when no user is in the conversation, the client authenticates as itself : it sends its client id and client secret to /token , and receives a token representing the service , not a person. classic uses: a cron job calling an admin api, a backend calling a payment provider. the consequence to internalize: the token's authority is whatever that service is allowed to do — if you hand scope: admin to a client credentials client, you've given a background job admin rights with no human in the middle. device flow: when there's no browser at all the device gets a user code and a verification url; the user approves on any device; the device polls until the token is ready. this is how "sign in with the code on your tv" works — and the tester relevant detail is that the device must keep polling at a sane interval and stop once authorized or expired. refresh tokens: extending a session safely access tokens are short lived; a refresh token lets a server side client get a new one without making the user re login. the discipline: the refresh token lives server side never in browser storage , is rotated on each use a reused one is a sign of theft and gets revoked , and is itself revocable on logout. "refresh token rotation" is the behavior your tester should poke at hardest. picking and testing the right grant tester checklist: pkce : replay a stolen authorization code without the verifier — it must be rejected; reuse the verifier on two codes — second must fail. client credentials : confirm the token's scopes are the minimum the service needs an admin scope on a background job is a finding . refresh rotation : use a refresh token twice — the second use must be denied rotation + reuse detection . device flow : confirm polling stops after expiry, the code expires, and the user's approval grants only the requested scopes. no password grant : if you see grant type=password in logs or docs, that's legacy tech debt to flag, not copy. related systems oauth 2.0 and openid connect ../oauth2 openid connect — the authorization code flow in end to end detail http authentication and basic auth ../http authentication basic auth — the header these tokens ride in jwt tokens ../jwt tokens — what the access token usually is, and how to verify it single sign on sso ../single sign on sso — the flow behind "log in once everywhere"5d:T1540,oracle cloud infrastructure (oci) the oracle cloud map: compartments, vcn, compute/exadata, autonomous database, oci object storage, and iam — and how oracle database fits natively in oci unlike anywhere else. cloud-engineer data-architect solution-architect backend cloud oracle database architecture  what this system is oracle cloud infrastructure oci is oracle's public cloud — its reason to exist is running oracle database . where aws and azure are generic clouds, oci is the only one where a managed oracle database autonomous database / exadata is a first class native service rather than a port. the rest of oci vcns, compute, storage, iam exists to support that core use case — and increasingly to host generic workloads too. everything in oci hangs off tenancy → compartment organization. you don't deploy "into a region" so much as "into a compartment inside your tenancy," with iam policies deciding who can touch which compartment tree. the oci mental model concept what it is the closest aws/azure idea tenancy your root isolation boundary in oci aws account / azure subscription root compartment logical container for resources; iam grants access to compartments resource group / organizational unit region + ad region = data center geography; availability domains inside region / availability zone vcn your virtual network, sliced into subnets vpc / vnet oci iam policies allow group x to manage ... in compartment y that are statement based , not role based iam policies permission based / azure rbac oci object storage buckets with namespaces; oci s3 compatible api s3 / blob storage the big oci difference: iam is statement based, written in plain language. you write allow group app team to manage all resources in compartment app prod — it reads like a sentence and composes from the root. the failure mode of this is overly permissive sentences written once and copied forever. compartments and iam: the tree you govern policies attach to compartments; resources inherit placement inside a compartment's tree. rules of thumb: one compartment per cost owner , never put prod and non prod under the same grant, and put the database pool in its own compartment so "manage app" never means "manage the db." the real oci: running oracle database natively offering what it is when you reach for it autonomous database adb s fully managed oracle db: auto patching, auto backup, auto scaling, tooling for dbas is
